| G210 | **A rule that depends on a fact no manifest carries must decline to decide, not pass.** `policy reach --cni` answers one reachability question; the policy being inert is a property of the bundle, so KN-N014 reports it in the audit, where a CI gate can fail on it -- HIGH, for the reason KN-S030 is HIGH: a policy gate that silently stopped applying is worse than one that was never there, because somebody is relying on it. The design turns on the three-valued machinery G202 built. No manifest states which CNI runs the cluster, so WITHOUT `--cni` the rule registers `cannot_decide` and the coverage record reports it under not-evaluated; counting it clean would be reporting a check that never ran, and "nobody told me the CNI" is not "the policy is enforced". The same holds for a CNI upstream does not characterise and for a version too coarse to sit on either side of the removal (G209). Two boundaries that are NOT cannot_decide: a bundle with no admin policy at all was evaluated and clean here, because nothing to decide is not a failure to decide -- reversed by G281, which records it as not evaluated with the absent kinds named, since nothing to decide is not a pass either; and an enforcing CNI is evaluated and clean. The rule generates no admission policy and is skipped by the webhook, because the CNI and its version are not in any admitted object -- recorded in `NOT_GENERATED` rather than left to be discovered. The per-rule fixture format gained an optional `cni:` key for the same reason: without it the bad fixture cannot fire, and exempting the rule from "every rule ships a good and a bad fixture that fires" would have weakened the invariant instead of the fixture | `tests/test_audit_cni.py::test_a_policy_the_named_cni_ignores_is_reported`, `tests/test_audit_cni.py::test_with_no_cni_the_rule_declines_to_decide_rather_than_passing`, `tests/test_audit_cni.py::test_an_undocumented_cni_also_declines_to_decide`, `tests/test_audit_cni.py::test_a_coarse_version_declines_to_decide`, `tests/test_audit_cni.py::test_a_bundle_with_no_admin_policy_is_not_evaluated_and_says_why`, `tests/test_audit_cni.py::test_an_enforcing_cni_is_clean_and_evaluated`, `tests/test_audit_cni.py::test_both_kinds_are_reported`, `tests/test_audit_cni.py::test_the_finding_fails_a_gate`, `tests/test_audit_cni.py::test_the_cli_reports_it_and_a_bad_value_is_refused`, `tests/test_audit_cni.py::test_the_tool_schema_documents_the_flag` |
| G211 | **A job timeout is not a diagnosis.** The `videos` job was cancelled at 30m19s on a run whose other 19 checks were green and whose own steps reported no failure, so the only thing the 30 minutes said was "something took too long". Reproducing it locally with the pinned Playwright 1.63.0 measured the whole render at about 90 seconds -- voiceover 7-17s per video, `capture.js --check` 6-9s, the stills 5-7s, the walkthrough and thumbnail 11s, 23 stills out -- which rules out the render and points at the install step, where `playwright install --with-deps` runs apt-get and an apt prompt on a runner with no tty waits forever. So: `DEBIAN_FRONTEND=noninteractive` with `sudo -E` (plain `sudo` drops the environment, which would have made the fix a no-op), and a `timeout-minutes` on every step, so the next hang fails with its own name instead of eating the budget. The test that pins it checks the budgets SUM under the job's own limit too, and caught the first attempt at 32 against 30: three bounded steps that can still only die at the job boundary are not bounded. **The bounded step then fired, which is the point, and a 12-minute step that dies at its own limit still only says "something in here hangs".** So it is one command per step now: pip, apt, npm ci, the browser download. Measured off the runner, pip is 20s and `npm ci` is 3s, which leaves the two commands that cannot be measured here -- and both apt hangs are now closed rather than guessed between. `DEBIAN_FRONTEND` answers the prompt-with-no-tty case; `-o DPkg::Lock::Timeout=180` answers the dpkg lock unattended-upgrades holds, where apt's default is to wait with no limit at all. And `--with-deps` is gone: it runs apt a SECOND time inside Playwright's own sudo, where neither of those reaches it. The image carries Chromium's libraries, and a missing one now fails the render in seconds naming the library -- a loud failure in place of a silent one | `tests/test_video_workflows.py::test_every_step_of_the_ci_videos_job_is_bounded_on_its_own`, `tests/test_video_workflows.py::test_apt_can_neither_prompt_nor_wait_forever_for_the_dpkg_lock`, `tests/test_video_workflows.py::test_the_browser_install_does_not_shell_out_to_apt_again` |

An explicit `--cni` wins over the read, because a caller who passes it knows something a forbidden read cannot see. **And writing the tests reversed a decision from G209.** That entry stopped reading a tier the CNI ignores, to keep a 403 on it from withholding the verdict -- but not reading it also loses the inert finding, which is the most valuable thing in the output: "this AdminNetworkPolicy is in your cluster and decides nothing" needs the policy in hand. | G215 | **A limit that drops output silently has to be spent on saying so.** `gate --sarif` wrote SARIF and `action.yml` ran the gate, but nothing turned a finding into an annotation, so a finding landed in a log a developer had to go looking for. GitHub's own caps shape the fix: "10 warning annotations, 10 error annotations, and 10 notice annotations per step" and "50 annotations per job" (actions/toolkit, docs/problem-matchers.md, retrieved 2026-11-05). One annotation per finding would mean the eleventh is dropped **with no message**, and a developer who sees ten annotations reasonably concludes there are ten problems -- a count that stops being true, which is this repository's most-repeated defect. So nine findings are annotated and the tenth slot of each kind carries the number that is not shown and points at the SARIF and the job summary. An annotation is an error when it is a finding the gate failed on and a warning otherwise, which is not the same as being at or above `--fail-on`: the first version read the severity, and G216 records the two wrong verdicts that produced. The escaping follows the toolkit's rules, and the properties escape a comma and a colon pair while the message does not need to -- the message is the rest of the line. | G216 | **Red-teaming G214 and G215 found seven defects, and two of them were wrong verdicts -- the same class the two rows were written to close.** (1) `snapshot()` filtered the collected objects down to the kinds it tracks *before* the rules saw them, so a bundle holding a ResourceQuota was audited as if it had none and KN-C016 fired on an absence that was not there; the rules now see every object with a `kind`, and tracking and auditing are separate questions. (2) A snapshot whose reads were refused counted the rules that never ran as `clean` -- a forbidden `roles` read published "AC-6 16 clean". The engine knows which rules fire on an absence but not which kind each rule reads, so no `clean` on a partial snapshot can be trusted: standing is now withheld entirely, with the reason, whenever `notRead` is non-empty, and the audit runs with `partial_bundle=True` so absence rules decline rather than fire. The `failures` reasons are annotated first and share the one ten-error budget. (5) The standing table dropped the `excepted` and `withheld` columns its own note explains, so a critical finding under an exception rendered as a clean row. (6) The twenty-namespace cap truncated the list with no count -- the G215 defect repeated one file over. (7) A property value ending in `:` ate the `::` separator. | G217 | **A crosswalk onto someone else's register has to be generated, because the register moves and the document does not.** `docs/compliance.md` promised a FedRAMP 20x KSI mapping and described, in prose, the file it would be built from: `FRMR.KSI.key-security-indicators.json` at release 25.11C, eleven families, indicators keyed by number, seven of them `retired: true` -- and it said evidence offered against a withdrawn indicator is worse than none, which was the right instinct aimed at the wrong facts. Every one of those specifics was false. FedRAMP consolidated its rules into one dataset (`fedramp-consolidated-rules.json`, version 2026.11.02.01, `last_updated` 2026-11-02): **ten** families, **46** indicators keyed by mnemonic (`KSI-CED-RAT`), every family `status: stable`, and **no** retirement, withdrawal or deprecation flag anywhere in it -- so the seven ids recorded as retired no longer exist as ids at all and could not be updated, only deleted. The crosswalk now lives inside each indicator as `controls: [lower-case NIST ids]`, so `kube-noob ksi` is that list inverted through the same transformation `oscal.control_id` already uses, and both the module and `docs/fedramp-ksi.md` are generated from a vendored extract whose sha256 is asserted offline. Three things the shape of this feature is deliberately built around: a control no indicator cites keeps its row with an empty tuple (`SC-6`, `SC-36`) because a crosswalk listing only what matched reads as full coverage; an indicator no kube-noob rule touches reports `evidenced: false` with no counts at all, because a zero in a findings column reads as a pass; and `varies_by_class` is not a flag but a block of per-class statements, one of which is marked optional, so the classes are carried rather than collapsed into a boolean. The curated half is 13 indicators with one line on what the evidence shows and one on what it does not -- `KSI-CNA-RNT` is evidence that policies exist, not that a CNI enforces them (G210); `KSI-SVC-VRI` that images are pinned, not that a signature verifies; `KSI-RPL-ABO` says nothing about backups. The caveat that a mapping is evidence and never a compliance claim is kube-noob's own sentence, not a quotation: the consolidated dataset makes no such statement and attributing one to FedRAMP would be this item's own failure mode. | G218 | **A bound set from the measurements you have is a guess about the one you do not.** The `videos` job was split into bounded steps so a hang would name itself (G211), and the comment beside those budgets said in so many words that apt and the browser download were the two things it could not measure off a runner -- and then gave apt four minutes anyway. On 2026-11-05 at 21:19Z the Azure Ubuntu mirror served `libflite1` (13.6 MB) in 62 seconds, the step was still downloading when the bound killed it, and a commit whose other 19 checks were green got a red `ci/videos`; the re-run passed in 2m12s, which is the signature of someone else's slow day rather than a defect. The bound stays, because an unbounded apt is the original hang, but it is now above the slowest transfer actually observed instead of below it, and the job's own limit went to 36 so the step budgets still fit inside it. Two cheaper things come first: the step skips apt entirely when the runner image already ships ffmpeg, and apt is told to retry a dropped connection itself. The retry loop is the part worth testing -- a second attempt that swallowed a failure would report success with no ffmpeg installed, and that lands three steps later as an unexplained render failure, which is the diagnosis problem this job was split up to solve, one step further along. | G219 | **A shared control is not shared subject matter, and prose written from control names is not evidence.** A red team on G217 reproduced 25 defects. The eight worst were all in the part I hand-wrote: the curated sentences described each indicator's evidence from the CONTROL names rather than from the rules, and were checked against neither. `KSI-CNA-MAT` ("Minimizing Attack Surface") claimed privileged containers, host namespaces, hostPath and added capabilities -- the first four things anyone would call attack surface, and not one of them reaches an indicator citing `sc-7.5`, `sc-8` and `si-10`. `KSI-SVC-SIN` claimed a volume-encryption rule that does not exist in the catalogue at all. `KSI-SCR-MIT` claimed scanner evidence that cannot arrive, because a scanner finding carries the CVE id as its rule id and no control maps it. The structural fix is that every sentence now names the rule ids it is about, a test refuses an id that is not behind that indicator or not in the catalogue, a second test refuses an id the prose does not mention, and the generated page prints each rule's own title beside the sentence so a reader can check the two agree. The deeper finding is the one worth keeping: `KSI-IAM-SNU` is about how non-user credentials are issued, it cites `ac-4` (Information Flow Enforcement), and kube-noob maps NetworkPolicy rules to `ac-4` because that is what `ac-4` is -- so eight traffic and RBAC rules land under an authentication indicator and the join is arithmetically correct and about nothing. Those entries are now marked `onSubject: False` and say so in text, in JSON and in the page. Separately, I attributed the applicability quotation to the dataset's `info.description`, which contains nothing of the kind; it is in the FedRAMP/rules README, and `info` is not vendored so no check could have caught the misattribution -- attributing a statement to FedRAMP that FedRAMP did not make being this feature's own declared failure mode. The rest were the command taking a shortcut past the input pipeline `audit` uses: a Helm values file was scored instead of refused (31 least-privilege rules "clean" on a file no rule can read), one file counted the absent-object rules as clean instead of not evaluated (G202 through a second door, and the mirror image too -- reporting "no NetworkPolicy" against a file the policy was simply not in), an unreadable file in a directory was dropped where `audit` exits 5 and names it, an empty directory produced a page, the audit's own assumptions were dropped, a finding whose rule reaches no indicator vanished, `withheld: 0` was asserted without a fired list (G199), a filter hid 18 rows silently (G215), and `indicators_for("ac-6")` answered `()` -- the same answer as the honest `()` for `SC-6`, from which a caller could conclude there is no evidence for a control with thirty rules behind it | | G220 | **A crosswalk only the CLI can reach is half-shipped.** N3 landed as `kube-noob ksi` and nothing else, in a project whose main surface is an MCP server and a Claude Code plugin -- so the one output an assistant would be asked for during a compliance conversation was the one it could not call. `fedramp_ksi` is now a tool, read-only, with `manifests` optional. Two things the second front door forced into the open. First, `partial_bundle` defaults to **true** here and to false in `audit_manifests`, because the inputs differ in kind: a path on disk is usually what the user means by the whole of it, and a manifest pasted into a conversation is usually a fragment -- defaulting to false there would report "no NetworkPolicy selects this workload" against a namespace whose policy the caller simply did not paste (G202's shape again, from the other side). Second, ruff caught both validation branches raising an undefined `ConfigError` before any test did: the tests exercised the refusal that comes from `refuse_if_not_manifests` and neither of the two I had written myself, so a caller passing `KSI-CMT-05` -- the id shape the previous dataset used, and therefore the likeliest wrong input -- would have got a `NameError`. A test now covers each branch, and one more holds the tool and the command to the same answer on the same input, because two front doors that disagree leave a reader unable to tell which is wrong | `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_the_crosswalk_is_a_tool_and_is_read_only`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_the_tool_answers_without_manifests`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_the_tool_refuses_what_the_audit_tool_refuses`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_the_tool_agrees_with_the_command_on_the_same_input`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_the_tool_carries_the_partial_bundle_question_to_the_caller`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_the_tool_rejects_an_id_that_does_not_exist` |
| G221 | **The caveat has to travel with the counts, in the format the caller actually reads.** A second red team found ten more defects, and the worst was that G219's whole repair -- every curated sentence saying what the evidence does not show -- lived in the CLI's text renderer. The tool returns JSON and nothing else, so an assistant saw `KSI-SVC-VRI: clean 3` with no sign that kube-noob fetches no signature and verifies nothing, and the tool description's promise that two rows "carry a warning" was simply false in JSON. Both now come from the row itself and the text view renders from the row, so one source feeds both and they cannot drift. Five more were absences reading as passes, in the same place each time: the crosswalk-only reply asserted `findings: 0` on 28 evidenced rows although nothing had been audited (counts are now absent unless something was, with an explicit `audited` flag); `manifests=""` returned that same reassuring crosswalk because the model strips whitespace and the code tested truthiness, so an empty file was indistinguishable from no file; a target past the removal table's ceiling dropped the audit's own `notCheckedBeyond`, which is precisely the caveat on SA-22's evidence; a declining rule was a bare `notEvaluated: 1`, so the KN-N014 that knows an AdminNetworkPolicy may be inert was invisible AND unreachable, there being no `cni` on either front door; and `rules: []` beside `evidenced: true` read as "no rules behind this" when it meant "none fired" -- now `rulesFired` and `rulesBehind`. Two were beneath the feature, in the engine, and both affect `audit_manifests` as much as this: KN-ST001 skipped its one cross-object branch under `partial_bundle` WITHOUT declining, so it stayed in `evaluated_rules` and was counted clean while the same report's assumptions said an undeclared StorageClass was not evaluated; and `yaml.SafeLoader(text)` sat outside its own exception handler, so a control character in a manifest -- the Reader validates the whole string in the constructor -- escaped as an unhandled YAMLError and the server returned 500 "internal error" for input it simply could not parse. A caller can act on a 400 and not on a 500. Last, the tool ignored `defaultPlatform` while the command honoured it, so on a configured server the two doors disagreed by six indicators' worth of KN-V003, a finding that is an artefact of the wrong platform; `platform` is optional now and falls back to the setting | `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_every_row_carries_the_prose_not_just_the_counts`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_the_crosswalk_alone_asserts_no_counts`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_an_evidenced_row_says_how_many_rules_are_behind_it`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_a_declining_rule_is_named_so_the_caller_can_supply_what_it_wanted`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_a_target_past_the_removal_table_is_not_a_clean_result`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_blank_manifests_are_refused_rather_than_crosswalked`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_the_tool_uses_the_configured_default_platform`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_the_text_view_omits_the_excepted_column_when_no_list_was_read`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_the_partial_bundle_advice_fits_both_front_doors`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_a_control_character_is_refused_not_an_internal_error`, `tests/test_fedramp_ksi.py::test_an_undeclared_storage_class_is_not_clean_on_a_partial_bundle` |
| G222 | **The first HTML kube-noob has ever produced, and the rules that make it trustworthy rather than merely prettier (U1).** Until now `--format` was text, json or yaml and the only HTML the code touched was web content it *read*; the audience for per-control standing and the KSI crosswalk is a compliance reviewer, and what a reviewer got was terminal text they cannot forward or JSON they will not read. Three constraints, each one a test rather than an intention. The page fetches nothing: no stylesheet, script, font or image, verified by parsing the output for every attribute that makes a browser load something and again by rendering it in Chromium with the request log checked. The caveats come before the findings, because a reader who meets an empty findings table first has already concluded -- `audited: false`, `assumed`, `notCheckedBeyond`, an unreadable file, a withheld standing and the shows/does-not-show pair all survive, since an HTML report that drops them is G221 in a third format. And nothing from a manifest is ever markup: this is the one place in the product where someone else's resource names become a document a third person opens, so every value is escaped and a Deployment named `<script>alert(1)</script>` renders as visible, inert text. The backtick-to-code-span conversion escapes first and only then looks for backticks, so the only markup it can find is markup this code wrote. Two defects the first render showed that no test had: the byline read "by kube-noob 2026.11.02.01" because the version fell back to the FedRAMP dataset's -- a false claim about the tool in the first line of a document written for someone checking claims -- and an unevidenced indicator's explanatory note sat in the Findings column, which is the one column on that page a reader must not misread. Looking at the thing found both; the test suite found neither | `tests/test_report_html.py::test_the_page_fetches_nothing_and_runs_nothing`, `tests/test_report_html.py::test_a_resource_name_is_never_rendered_as_markup`, `tests/test_report_html.py::test_every_caveat_the_text_view_carries_survives`, `tests/test_report_html.py::test_the_caveats_come_before_the_findings`, `tests/test_report_html.py::test_a_withheld_standing_says_why_instead_of_showing_a_table`, `tests/test_report_html.py::test_an_unaudited_ksi_page_shows_no_counts`, `tests/test_report_html.py::test_the_byline_never_borrows_another_version`, `tests/test_report_html.py::test_backticks_become_code_spans_and_hostile_text_stays_inert`, `tests/test_report_html.py::test_a_row_with_no_counts_does_not_sit_under_the_findings_heading` |
| G223 | **A release is a set of claims that all have to agree, and the ones about the release itself go stale first.** Cutting 0.8.0 means eight files carrying a version -- the package, `server.json`, the marketplace entry, the plugin manifest and its pyproject, the chart's `version` and `appVersion`, and the krew manifest -- and the release workflow refuses a tag that disagrees with any of them, which is the right shape: the check is at the gate, not in a reviewer's head. `scripts/bump_version.py` moves all of them and `scripts/krew_manifest.py` and `scripts/sbom.py` regenerate what hangs off the version. What no generator covered were two prose claims about the project's own release history: the website queue's "As of ... version 0.7.1" (caught, because a test already holds that line against the code) and MAINTENANCE.md's "13 releases in five days" (not caught, because nothing held it against the tags). The second now has a test that counts `git tag`, with exactly one release of slack, because the tag for the release being prepared does not exist until the maintainer cuts it. Writing that test reproduced G220 in miniature: it passed on the first run and ruff then found an undefined `pytest` in its skip branch, which the run had never entered -- a second time in one week that the linter caught a name the tests could not | `tests/test_doc_claims.py::test_the_release_cadence_claim_counts_the_tags_that_exist`, `tests/test_guidance.py::test_the_website_queue_states_the_real_numbers` |
| G224 | **An annotation anchored to a path the checkout does not have is worse than no annotation: it looks reported and is not.** A red team recorded this on 2026-11-05 as unverified and I carried it forward; re-checking it while cutting 0.8.0 reproduced it in one call. `gate -f -` gives every finding the uri `-`, and `--github-annotations` then emitted 11 commands saying `file=-`, asking GitHub to anchor them to a file named `-`. The runner drops those from the diff or places them meaninglessly, so the pull request shows fewer findings than the gate found while the step looks like it reported them. The fix is to anchor only to a path a checkout could have and otherwise leave the annotation at repository level, where it is still visible. Two things worth keeping from the audit that found it: re-verifying beats carrying a list forward -- of the three items I had down as open from an earlier pass, two (KN-C016 order-dependence, and an exception filing reported as a fixed finding) no longer reproduce at all and had been fixed by later work without anyone closing them out, and the third was this one, real | `tests/test_github_annotations.py::test_an_annotation_is_not_anchored_to_a_path_no_checkout_has` |
| G225 | **Guessing which half of a contradictory input the caller meant is inventing an answer.** `--platform kubernetes --version 4.22` was taken at face value as Kubernetes minor "4.22", a release that does not exist; every version-gated rule then answered about it, and only the beyond-the-table caveat (G202's cousin) kept that from being a silent wrong answer rather than a loud one. The obvious repair -- map 4.22 through the OpenShift table -- is worse than the bug, because it would also silently change which rules apply: KN-V003 exists only on OpenShift. So it is refused, with both readings named in the message. The asymmetry is deliberate and recorded rather than quietly removed: an OpenShift target given a 1.x version still accepts it, because that direction loses no rules and the behaviour predates this. Found by re-verifying a red team's "judgement call" list instead of filing it | `tests/test_doc_claims.py::test_an_openshift_version_on_the_kubernetes_platform_is_refused` |
| G226 | **The first honest check in the logging family, and the discipline that made it smaller than it looked.** Two outside reviews put audit logging and SIEM at the top of kube-noob's missing coverage and proposed about twelve rules each. Eleven of the twelve need credentials for a system this project holds none for, so they could only ever answer "not evaluated": coverage theatre. The twelfth is real -- OpenShift keeps audit settings in a cluster-scoped `APIServer` singleton that ordinary read-only RBAC can read -- and `KN-S035` reads it. Verified against openshift/api `config/v1/types_apiserver.go` before a line was written: cluster-scoped, named `cluster`, `spec.audit.profile` one of Default, WriteRequestBodies, AllRequestBodies or None, and `customRules` a per-group list evaluated top to bottom **whose first match takes precedence over the top-level profile**. That last clause is the finding worth having: a cluster reporting `profile: Default` can log nothing for a named group, so it looks audited and is not -- this repository's own defect class, sitting in someone else's API. What happened next is the part to remember. The rule brought `AU-2` and `AU-12` into the control catalogue, and the crosswalk promptly moved THREE indicators off the unevidenced list: KSI-MLA-LET, KSI-MLA-OSM and KSI-MLA-RVL. It has business claiming exactly one. LET asks which event types are logged, which is the rule's subject; OSM asks whether a SIEM is operated and RVL whether anyone reads the logs, and both arrive only because FedRAMP and kube-noob both name `au-2`. Marking them `onSubject: False` is the whole of G219 working: adding a rule honestly made the coverage claim smaller, not larger. Six guards caught the consequences -- the krew manifest, the scorecard, the website queue, the README and brand counts, the OSCAL id map (ids confirmed present in catalogue 5.2.0 / OSCAL 1.2.3 before adding them, not assumed), and the safety contract, which refused the new cluster read until the chart's RBAC granted it | `tests/test_audit_profile.py::test_a_custom_rule_that_logs_nothing_for_one_group_is_a_finding`, `tests/test_audit_profile.py::test_a_bundle_without_the_object_declines_rather_than_passing`, `tests/test_audit_profile.py::test_the_indicators_it_reaches_say_which_one_it_is_actually_about`, `tests/test_audit_profile.py::test_the_cluster_reader_knows_the_resource`, `tests/test_safety_contract.py::test_the_chart_read_rules_mirror_the_backends_allowlist`, `tests/test_oscal.py::test_every_control_resolves_to_the_pinned_nist_catalogue_id` |
| G227 | **CodeQL was wrong about the category and right about the code.** It flagged `api_version.startswith("config.openshift.io/")` in KN-S035 as incomplete URL sanitization. The framing does not hold -- `apiVersion` is a Kubernetes group/version string, not a URL -- and the alert was not exploitable either, because the trailing slash makes it a true prefix: `config.openshift.io.attacker.example/v1` never matched it. The temptation was therefore to dismiss it. But a prefix match is the wrong tool for an API group, which is a field with a separator and an exact value, so the check now splits on `/` and compares the group. Same behaviour for every input that matters, clearer intent, and the alert goes away because the construct it pattern-matched on is gone rather than suppressed. A test pins both halves -- a look-alike group decides nothing, and any served version of the real group is read -- so the property survives the next person who finds the split verbose. The general lesson: a scanner's classification can be wrong while its finger is on something worth changing, and "the alert is a false positive" is an argument about the label, not about the line | `tests/test_audit_profile.py::test_only_the_real_api_group_is_read` |
| G228 | **A release step that imports the package needs the package's dependencies, and a second `setup-python` silently takes them away.** v0.8.0's release failed at `krew_manifest.py --check` with `No module named 'yaml'`. Nothing was published -- PyPI returned 404 for 0.8.0 -- so the version survived, which is the only reason this is a CI bug and not a burned release number. The cause is an ordering nobody would spot by reading: `build-dist` calls `setup-python` twice, once to build and once to match the interpreter inside the pinned PyPI publish action, and every step after that second switch runs on a fresh Python with no project dependencies. It had never mattered, because no step after the switch imported `kube_noob` -- until #42 added a rule count to the krew plugin's description, which pulls in `yamlio` and so PyYAML. The last several releases passing is what made it invisible: the import arrived in a commit whose own CI was entirely green, because `ci.yml` installs the package and `release.yml` is the only workflow that does not. Fixed by installing the same hash-locked `requirements.lock` the action itself installs, so the scripts run against the dependency set that ships rather than whatever resolves on the day. Verified both directions in clean virtual environments before pushing: bare interpreter reproduces the ModuleNotFoundError, locked install passes both `--check`s. The guard asserts the ordering rather than the symptom -- after the last interpreter switch, no step may import the package before a step has installed its dependencies -- because the next script added to that job will have the same problem and a test for "yaml is importable" would not have caught it | `tests/test_distribution.py::test_the_release_installs_dependencies_before_importing_the_package` |
| G229 | **The pod specs that are not at `spec.template.spec`, and the blind spot that read as clean.** A KServe `InferenceService` running a privileged, root container with `hostPath: /` and a mutable tag produced **14 findings as a Deployment and zero as an InferenceService** -- not "not evaluated", clean. Every pod-level rule in the catalogue, and the whole attack-path scan, read pod specs only from the nine ordinary workload paths, so an operator CRD that embeds its own pod spec was invisible to all of them: the exact defect class G219-G228 exist to prevent, this time caused by a reader's silence rather than a rule's verdict. Two registries fix it, and the discipline is in how they were filled. Every path was read out of the **published CRD schema** on 2026-11-06, not inferred from a tutorial: KServe's `predictor`, `transformer` and `explainer` are each a bare inline PodSpec (the schema carries all eleven PodSpec fields, `hostNetwork`, `hostPID` and `hostIPC` among them) from `config/crd/full/serving.kserve.io_inferenceservices.yaml`, and KubeRay's `headGroupSpec.template` and each `workerGroupSpecs[].template` are ordinary PodTemplateSpecs, with RayService wrapping them under `rayClusterConfig` and RayJob under `rayClusterSpec`. A second registry pins the API group of each kind, so a `RayCluster` in someone else's group is left alone (G227) -- while a kind with **no** `apiVersion` is read, because both live collectors set only `kind` and API list items carry no apiVersion of their own, so refusing an absent group would have restored the blind spot for every `--live` scan. The attack-path scan can score such a component's node and API capabilities but often cannot trace an entry edge to it, which it says in `limitations` naming the component -- silence there would have read as "not exposed", the same failure one level up. And the fix is deliberately smaller than the problem: four kinds with verified schemas, not every CRD that might carry a pod, because a guessed path is a wrong finding and a wrong finding is worse than a known gap. **Two claims in the first version of this row were wrong and are corrected by G231**: that reading `containers` was enough for a KServe component, and that its `labels()` should return nothing. Both were reasoned from the shape of the API instead of from its published schema and its controller's source, which is the thing this row says not to do | `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_an_inference_service_is_audited_not_passed`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_every_pod_level_rule_that_fires_on_a_deployment_fires_on_a_ray_cluster`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_same_named_kind_from_another_group_is_not_read`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_kind_with_no_api_version_is_read_because_the_live_collectors_drop_it`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_kserve_component_carries_the_labels_the_controller_actually_sets`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_the_scan_reaches_a_ray_head_and_every_worker_group`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_an_unmatchable_component_is_named_as_undetermined_not_left_silent`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_every_embedded_kind_is_in_the_read_only_allowlist` |
| G230 | **The one document that measures the whole product was the one the generator did not cover.** `scripts/doc_numbers.py` exists because four typed numbers were wrong at once (G212), and it owns two regions of two files. `docs/roadmap.md` -- the file whose job is to state what is built, measured rather than estimated -- was not one of them, and by 2026-11-06 it said "91 rules" and "28 of NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5's controls" on a catalogue of 92 whose thirtieth control had just got a rule, in a sentence that opens "Measured, not estimated". Its "105 commands" matched no counting method at all: the parser has 108 subcommand nodes and 93 leaves, so whatever produced 105 is unreproducible, which is worse than a number that is merely out of date. Six figures corrected, and all of them generated now, each from the registry that defines it -- `list_rules`, `Toolkit.specs`, the parser walked for every subcommand node at any depth, the chart's templates, `RULE_CONTROLS` -- with the script stating what each one counts so a later change cannot quietly redefine one while `--check` still passes, which a second test pins independently of the generator. The part worth keeping is the one the first attempt got wrong: reporting the line counts exactly made the document stale the moment this very guidance row's test was added, so every commit that adds a test would have failed the gate, which is how a gate teaches people to ignore it. They are rounded to the nearest thousand and the sentence says so. A measurement whose precision exceeds its stability is not a stronger claim, it is a flakier one | `tests/test_doc_claims.py::test_the_roadmap_states_the_measured_numbers`, `tests/test_doc_claims.py::test_the_measured_sentence_counts_the_catalogue_the_audit_runs`, `tests/test_doc_claims.py::test_the_committed_documents_are_current`, `ci:Documented measurements match what the commands produce` |
| G231 | **Red-teaming the fix found the same defect one level further down, twice, plus two regressions the fix itself introduced.** G229 taught the readers four CRDs and claimed the blind spot closed. An adversarial pass over it, with the published schemas downloaded and walked rather than remembered, found that the fix missed **the form almost every KServe manifest in the world uses**: a predictor is written `model: {modelFormat: ..., storageUri: ...}`, and the schema makes `model` -- and the ten framework shorthands `sklearn`, `xgboost`, `tensorflow`, `pytorch`, `triton`, `onnx`, `huggingface`, `pmml`, `lightgbm`, `paddle`, plus `explainer.art` -- each a full `v1.Container`, a FIELD of the component rather than an entry in its `containers` list. Reading only `containers` gave a privileged, root, `hostPath: /` predictor **2 findings where the identical spec as a list gave 11**, and the G229 bookkeeping now recorded every container rule as having evaluated it. The audit and the scan read containers through different functions, so the same input also scored no node capability at all. Three more unread pod specs came out of the same walk: KServe `predictor.workerSpec` and `canary[].predictor`, and KubeRay's `submitterPodTemplate` and its `autoscalerOptions`, a container the operator injects into the head pod. **The labels decision was wrong in the opposite direction, and that one shipped as a false positive.** G229 returned `{}` for a KServe component on the theory that the object's labels are not the pod's. KServe's own `buildObjectMeta` says they are: the pod's labels are the union of the ServingRuntime's, the object's, the component's `labels`, and `serving.kserve.io/inferenceservice` plus `component`. So the manifest-known set is a SUBSET of the real set, and `{}` merged "no labels" with "labels unknown" -- which made KN-V011 and KN-N012 tell a reader that a correct Service has no endpoints and a correct NetworkPolicy protects nothing. The fix is `selector_decides`, three-valued: a positive match on a subset stays a match on the whole, because `matchLabels`, `In` and `Exists` only ever require a label to be PRESENT, so True is trustworthy; a match resting on `NotIn` or `DoesNotExist` is undecided, because an unseen label falsifies it; and a non-match is undecided unless a label we can actually see contradicts the selector. Both rules now decline rather than fire, and declining removes them from the evaluated set, so "did not decide" cannot be published as "checked and clean". **The component ref broke a consumer**: `vuln threat` keys objects by `object_ref`, found no `RayCluster/ns/r` for the key `RayCluster/ns/r[headGroupSpec]`, and reported zero applications and zero threats for a Ray head the scan had just called internet-reachable. `Reach` carries the carrying object's ref as a field now, so the next consumer cannot get the parsing wrong. And four smaller ones, each the same shape: `sorted()` over a YAML mapping with an int key (`1:`) raised TypeError out of the audit, the scan and the admission webhook; KN-N001 was scoped to `LONG_RUNNING_KINDS`, so a namespace running only KServe or Ray was never asked for a NetworkPolicy and the rule was recorded as evaluated-clean on it; a same-named kind from a foreign group was refused in **silence**, where a Deployment with a made-up group gets KN-V002; and the new per-scan caveat reached the JSON reader only, which is G221 verbatim. The limitation's own predicate was wrong twice over -- "has no labels" told a plain label-less Pod that an operator sets its labels (false), and missed a labelled Ray head that is still unreachable from the manifest -- and its first wording said the capabilities were "scored above" when with no path nothing about them is printed above at all; it names them inline instead. The lesson is not that the reviewers were clever. It is that G229 was written from the shape of an API rather than from its published schema and its controller's source, in exactly the way G229's own row warns against, and that a change which makes a reader see more is also a change that makes every consumer of that reader's output wrong in a new way | `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_the_model_form_scores_the_same_as_the_containers_form`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_the_scan_scores_the_model_form_too`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_every_framework_shorthand_is_a_container`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_an_injected_autoscaler_container_is_audited`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_ray_job_submitter_pod_is_audited`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_correct_network_policy_is_not_called_useless`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_selector_that_contradicts_a_known_label_still_fires`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_declining_rule_is_not_recorded_as_evaluated`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_the_threat_model_still_sees_an_embedded_workload`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_mapping_with_mixed_key_types_does_not_crash_the_audit`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_namespace_of_only_serving_workloads_still_needs_a_network_policy`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_refused_api_group_is_said_out_loud`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_plain_pod_without_labels_is_not_told_an_operator_sets_them`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_kserve_component_carries_the_labels_the_controller_actually_sets` |
| G232 | **There are more workload CRDs than four, and a pod spec in a kind with no reader said nothing at all.** G229 and G231 taught the readers KServe and KubeRay. A sweep of seventeen other widely deployed CRDs -- Argo Rollout, Knative Service, Kubeflow Notebook, PyTorchJob/TFJob/MPIJob, Argo Workflow, Tekton, Volcano Job, JobSet, KEDA ScaledJob, Spark, Flink, Seldon, ECK, Prometheus, core `PodTemplate` -- found the same privileged, `hostPath: /` pod spec producing **zero findings in every one of them**, and `posture scan` reporting `0 workloads` for a file of Argo Rollouts, which is indistinguishable from a clean input. The tempting fix is to add seventeen registry entries. That is the wrong fix twice over: the paths would be written from how each API looks rather than from its published schema, which is precisely the mistake G231 exists to record, and the eighteenth CRD would be silent again anyway. The fix is to detect the SHAPE and report the gap: a mapping holding a `containers` list with at least one `image` is a pod spec in all but name, wherever it sits, and an object of an unreadable kind that contains one is reported as "N pod spec(s) are in kinds kube-noob has no reader for, so their containers were NOT audited", with the object and the path. The scan records the same as a `notRead` entry, because its kind filter drops such objects before anything else sees them. Narrowness is the whole design: `containers` whose entries have no `image`, a `containers` list of strings, and a ConfigMap whose VALUE is the word all stay quiet, and a kind the registries DO read is never also flagged, because there the registry is the claim and a second message would contradict it. One lesson from building it, worth more than the feature: the first draft capped its own walk at 24 levels while the loader accepts 100, so a pod spec nested deeper would have gone unmentioned -- the exact silent miss the detector exists to end, reintroduced inside the detector. It inherits the loader's depth limit now, and a test nests a pod spec past the old cap | `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_pod_spec_in_a_kind_with_no_reader_is_reported_not_passed`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_training_job_reports_every_replica_spec_it_could_not_read`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_an_object_that_merely_mentions_containers_is_not_accused`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_kind_the_registries_do_read_is_not_also_flagged`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_the_scan_says_it_dropped_a_workload_crd_rather_than_reporting_none`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_the_detector_looks_as_deep_as_the_loader_accepts` |
| G233 | **A kind name is unique only inside its group, and an exposure a CRD declares is still an exposure.** Two findings from the same sweep, opposite in direction. Use v1" -- as a **CRITICAL**, which fails a gate on a valid manifest. The same for a Volcano `Job`, and waiting for anyone's `Build`, `Task`, `Pipeline` or `Workflow`. A wrong CRITICAL costs more trust than a missed finding, and the fix is to say what the table is actually authoritative about: `KNOWN_API_GROUPS`, the groups the catalogue covers. Outside them the rule declines and the report's existing "no bundled schema" note stands, while every real case still fires, including the rule's own documented example of a Route in `apps.openshift.io`. In the other direction, a `RayCluster` with `headGroupSpec.serviceType: LoadBalancer` or `enableIngress: true` is asking the operator for a load balancer or an Ingress in front of its head pod -- the Ray dashboard and the job submission API, on a pod that is often privileged -- and because the Service that implements it is created by the operator and is never in the manifest, nothing matched a selector and `posture scan` answered **`0 attack paths from 0 exposures`**. An `Entry` can now name the object and pod spec path it lands on directly, and the three declared fields (verified in ray.io_ray{clusters,services,jobs}.yaml, v1 and v1alpha1) produce a real path with a real fix. Writing that step is the detail worth keeping: reusing the Service-selector step printed `Service/ns/ --selects--> ...` with the evidence "selector {} matches the pod template labels" -- a Service with no name and a sentence that was simply false. A synthesised edge needs synthesised evidence, or the report lies about where the path came from | `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_crd_reusing_a_core_kind_name_is_not_called_invalid`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_built_in_kind_in_the_wrong_group_still_fires`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_an_exposure_the_object_declares_is_an_exposure`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_cluster_ip_head_declares_nothing`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_declared_entry_does_not_invent_a_service_step` |
| G234 | **De-duplication by "has a controller" quietly deleted the workloads that matter most on a live cluster.** The attack-path scan skips controller-owned workloads so that a Deployment, its ReplicaSet and its Pods count once instead of three times. "Has a controller" is the wrong test for that. Everything a workload operator creates is controller-owned by its custom resource, so on a live KServe cluster the Deployment behind the predictor -- the one actually running the privileged container, selected by a LoadBalancer Service -- was dropped, and the scan reported nothing. `--live` is exactly what the scan's own limitation text tells a reader to try. Two conditions now, not one: the controller must be a kind whose own pod template carries the same spec (an ordinary workload kind, never a CRD whose spec this scan may not read at all), **and that owner must actually be in the input**. The second condition is the one that is easy to miss: dropping on the owner's kind alone would lose every ReplicaSet in a namespace scanned without its Deployments -- the same silent miss moved one object sideways. The cost of being wrong in the safe direction is one duplicated path; the cost of being wrong in the other is an internet-facing privileged pod that no report mentions. | G235 | **The product knew the answer and gave evidence-gathering steps instead.** `insight` is the front door: paste anything and it recognises what it is. Asked a question in WORDS about a job this product does, it ran the generic gather steps -- `get pods`, `describe pod`, `get events`, `logs --previous` -- and returned nothing. Measured on eighteen questions a platform engineer would actually type, **eight came back with no findings and no next step at all**, and "which of our exposed services could reach a node?" got pod-triage commands: steps that answer "something is broken, what is it", which is a different question from the one asked, while `posture scan` answers exactly that one and was never mentioned. For a tool whose adoption argument is "paste anything", a confident irrelevant answer is the worst available outcome. `CAPABILITY_ROUTES` maps the question to the command: eighteen routes, keyword-matched and deterministic -- no AI inside, which is the positioning -- surfaced as `answeredBy` beside the cluster steps rather than mixed into them, because those are commands against a cluster and these are commands against this product. Sixteen of the eighteen questions now reach the right command first; the other two already answered themselves with findings. The work was almost entirely in NOT over-matching, and every tightening came from a measured false positive: `gate` answered a Helm render error because "fail" was in both halves of its pattern; `posture scan` answered "can a pod in shop reach a pod in payments" because "pods" counted as a target, when that is a NetworkPolicy question; and the build analyser answered an `ImagePullBackOff` because "image" plus "failed" matched, when a runtime pull failure is not a build failure. Each needs two of the asking words, not one; sixteen pasted errors and a multi-line manifest stay silent; a question gets two suggestions at most, not a menu. The audit route's text states no rule count, because a number typed into a sentence is the G212 defect with a new hiding place. **Two claims in the first version of this row were false and are corrected by G237**: that every command named was checked against the CLI's parser (the check passed the longest real PREFIX, so `vuln rank` passed on `vuln` and eight of twenty printed invocations could not be run), and that pasted input stayed silent (nothing consulted what had been recognised, so a pasted Event whose pod was called `gate-7d9f` was answered with the CI gate) | `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_question_reaches_the_command_that_answers_it`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_every_routed_command_actually_parses`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_pasted_error_is_not_answered_with_a_command_for_something_else`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_pasted_manifest_or_log_is_never_routed`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_at_most_two_suggestions`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_the_insight_response_carries_the_routes_and_says_so`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_the_terminal_prints_them` |
| G236 | **`/metrics`, and the four decisions that make a metrics endpoint safe rather than merely present.** A platform team will not run a pod it cannot alert on, and until this the server had no `/metrics`, the chart had no PrometheusRule or ServiceMonitor, and the incident watch could parse an Alertmanager payload with nowhere for a receiver to post one -- a capability that existed and was unreachable. N11, and platform requirement R6.5's missing third. Instrumented at `run_tool`, the single point both the MCP tools and the REST routes pass through, so the two surfaces cannot report differently; no new dependency, because the exposition format is four fields per line and a hash-pinned library for that is a poor trade in a tool whose argument is that it runs disconnected. The decisions are the content. **Bounded labels:** `run_tool` labels an anticipated failure with the exception's class name, and exception types are open-ended, so a caller able to provoke a new one would add a Prometheus label value every time -- a cardinality attack on the monitoring system rather than on this process. Every label value is mapped into a fixed set first and `tool` is accepted only when the toolkit actually serves it. **Nothing about the caller:** tool, surface, outcome, duration. A scrape is readable by anyone who can reach the port and is kept for months, which makes it the last place customer data should appear, and a test asserts the label key set rather than trusting the review. **Guarded by wrapper order:** `/metrics` sits inside `BearerAuth` unless `server.metrics.allowUnauthenticated` says otherwise, because the document names every tool a deployment serves and how often each is called, which is operational intelligence about the team running it; opening the scrape must not open the tools, and a test checks that too. **Two dates, not one:** knowledge age is the IMAGE's review date, fixed at build time, and the corpus date exists only where a corpus is imported -- so they are separate metrics, the corpus one is absent rather than zero when there is none (a zero reads as 1970), and the alert on the first says in its own description that it is about the image and not a failed refresh. Two chart rules follow the same reasoning: both objects are off by default, because rendering a CRD the cluster does not serve fails the install, and enabling the PrometheusRule without the ServiceMonitor is **refused** -- an alert on a metric nobody scrapes never fires, and silence is indistinguishable from health. The error-ratio alert counts crash and error and deliberately not `invalid-arguments`, because a caller sending bad input is not this pod failing and paging someone for somebody else's typo teaches them to mute the alert. One guard is worth more than the rest: every `kube_noob_*` metric named anywhere in the PrometheusRule is checked against what the renderer actually emits, so an alert can never be written against a metric that does not exist | `tests/test_metrics.py::test_an_unknown_exception_type_collapses_to_error`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_an_unknown_tool_or_surface_does_not_become_a_label`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_no_label_value_can_carry_caller_data`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_metrics_needs_the_token_by_default`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_metrics_can_be_opened_deliberately`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_metrics_refuses_a_post`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_the_buckets_are_cumulative`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_knowledge_age_is_reported_and_the_corpus_date_only_when_there_is_one`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_every_alert_names_a_metric_this_code_emits`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_the_rule_refuses_without_a_service_monitor`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_the_error_ratio_alert_excludes_invalid_arguments`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_the_receiver_accepts_alertmanagers_own_payload_shape`, `ci:Chart guards (fail-closed NetworkPolicy, fsGroup only off-OpenShift, web flag wired to env)` |
| G237 | **A red team on the router found that nine of the twenty printed commands could not be run, and that the guard meant to prevent exactly that passed them.** G235's own row claimed every suggested command was checked against the CLI's parser. The test checked that the longest real PREFIX of the words was a command, which is the one-directional guard G206 is about: `kube-noob vuln rank` passed because `vuln` exists. Parsing all twenty showed eight that cannot run -- a missing `--port`, two missing positionals, three subcommands that do not exist (`vuln rank`, `build <log>`, `must-gather <path>`), a `-f` that means `--values`, and `generate policies`, which is a different command entirely -- plus `rhacs compare`, which parses and then exits 2 asking for `-f`. A suggestion that cannot be run is worse than no suggestion, and it was printed under the words "kube-noob answers this directly". The test parses them now. **Nothing consulted what had been recognised**, so a one-line pasted Event whose pod was called `gate-7d9f` was answered with the CI gate, a Deployment named `upgrade` with `upgrade-risks`, and an SBOM with a component called `gate` with the gate again -- while the tool description promised the field was "empty for anything pasted". It is gated on the recognised kinds now, as a SUBSET test rather than a membership test, because `recognise` returns no kind at all for several real typed questions and requiring the kind would have silenced those. **Three causes behind every remaining false answer, each worth more than its fix.** First, `\b` treats `-`, `/`, `:` and `.` as boundaries, so a route word inside a Kubernetes NAME matched it: a pod called `ci-runner-gate-5`, a Deployment called `upgrade-checker`, a namespace called `evidence`. The patterns are compiled with a boundary that requires a character which cannot appear in a name. Second, a word in TWO of a route's lookahead groups makes it fire on that one word -- which is the whole defect requiring two groups was meant to prevent. It had happened four times (`fail`, `gate`, `takeover` and `blast radius`, `evidence`), so it is refused at import now, and the first version of that refusal read `\bfail\b` as the word "bfail" and so could only ever fire by coincidence -- it still caught three real defects, which is how a guard that is right by accident goes unnoticed. Third, a sentence reporting a MALFUNCTION is a diagnosis whatever words it shares: "service ingress-nginx in namespace public crashes on the new node" is not an attack-path question. Deliberately excluding "fail" and "error" from that suppressor, because "my image build failed" and "why did my helm upgrade fail" are the two questions those routes exist for. **And the cost:** unanchored, `search` retried each pattern's lookaheads from every one of up to 600 start positions -- 75 to 92 ms per call on a 600-character line that matches nothing, paid on every one-line paste on every surface. Anchored, 1 ms. Anchoring first required finding a pattern whose alternation was written flat, so it parsed as `(?=(.*A)|(B)|(C))` and only worked because `search` scanned; anchored, two of its three branches would silently never have matched. Six routes were added for questions the sweep found had a precise answer and no route, two were reordered because the cap is two and table order decides which leads, six `why` strings that oversold their command were corrected, and the question with a direct answer no longer gets the pod-triage steps printed underneath it -- which was G235's own complaint, still true of its own output | `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_every_routed_command_actually_parses`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_nothing_is_routed_for_text_that_only_shares_words`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_no_word_is_in_two_lookahead_groups_of_a_route`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_every_route_is_anchored_so_the_cost_stays_linear`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_long_non_matching_line_is_cheap`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_recognised_artifact_is_not_routed_even_on_one_line`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_question_with_a_direct_answer_is_not_also_given_pod_triage_steps`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_the_better_answer_comes_first`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_carriage_returns_count_as_a_line_break`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_pasted_denial_is_left_to_the_explainer_that_already_ran` |
| G238 | **The command whose job is to close findings could not close them on an AI workload, and the reason was a dictionary copy.** G229 to G234 taught every READER about embedded pod specs. `fix` was not a reader in that sense: `harden_pod` took the one template at `spec.template.spec`, so the audit reported findings in a KServe predictor and `fix` left them there -- the two halves of the product disagreeing about what a pod is. It hardens every template now. Underneath it was a subtler defect of my own making: `containers()` yielded a field-shaped container (a `model:` block) as `{"name": key, **c}`, which is a **copy**, so the fixer's in-place hardening wrote into a dictionary the document had never seen and silently did nothing -- a fix that reports success and changes no file. `ContainerRef` carries the real object and the display name separately, and a test now reads the hardened field back out of the emitted YAML rather than trusting the change list. The lesson is that "teach the readers" is not finished when the audit agrees with itself: every consumer that re-derived a pod spec was a place the two halves could disagree, and the quietest of them was the one that mutated a copy | `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_fix_hardens_an_embedded_pod_spec`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_fix_writes_into_a_field_shaped_container_not_a_copy_of_it`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_mutable_tag_in_an_embedded_template_is_listed_as_manual_work`, `tests/test_embedded_podspecs.py::test_a_probe_on_an_embedded_template_counts_as_a_probe` |
| G239 | **A dashboard is a set of claims about what exists, so its queries are checked like any other claim.** U2 was blocked on N11 and stayed blocked on purpose: a panel referencing a metric that does not exist shows "No data" for ever, and nobody can tell that from a quiet system -- the same class of defect as a document quoting a count that stopped being true (G212). With `/metrics` shipped, `deploy/grafana/kube-noob.json` can be written, and the discipline transfers: a test extracts every `kube_noob_*` name from every panel's query and asserts the renderer emits it, including the `_bucket`/`_sum`/`_count` series a histogram implies. Three more properties that each come from a way dashboards usually go wrong. Every query is scoped to a `$job` the viewer picks, because a Grafana with several Prometheus sources otherwise sums somebody else's pod into these numbers. The datasource is a variable, not a uid, because a dashboard carrying the uid from the machine it was built on imports cleanly and shows nothing. And the failure-ratio panel is the SAME expression as the `KubeNoobToolErrorRatio` alert, asserted against the chart template, because a dashboard that disagrees with the page that woke someone at 3am is worse than no dashboard. The panel descriptions carry the two caveats the metrics themselves carry -- knowledge age is the image's age and not a failed refresh, and the counters are one process's -- so a reader who never opens the docs still gets them | `tests/test_metrics.py::test_every_dashboard_query_names_a_metric_this_code_emits`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_every_dashboard_query_is_scoped_to_the_selected_job`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_the_failure_ratio_panel_matches_the_alert`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_the_dashboard_has_a_datasource_variable_rather_than_a_hard_coded_uid`, `tests/test_metrics.py::test_the_dashboard_says_the_counters_are_per_process` |
| G240 | **Every grant that reads Secrets was reported the same way, and the one that reads their contents is not the same.** `KN-S017`'s Secret branch fired on `get`, `list`, `watch` and `*` with one message and one severity per role kind. `list` and `watch` return the objects' **contents**, so a single namespaced grant hands over the value of every Secret in that namespace; a `get` hands over the ones whose names the holder already knows. That was LOW either way. All three external reviews of the catalogue asked for exactly this distinction -- one of them wrote "remember that `list` can expose all secret values" -- and `docs/k8s-rbac` in this project's own bundled corpus has said "list returns contents" the whole time, which is the more embarrassing half. `list`/`watch` is now MEDIUM namespaced and HIGH cluster-wide, with a message that says what it hands over. The same branch turned out to have a false positive worth more than the refinement: a `get` narrowed by `resourceNames` is the least-privilege pattern the upstream RBAC guidance recommends, and the rule was reporting it as a privilege-escalation path -- telling someone their correct configuration is a defect, which is the one thing a security rule must not do. It is silent on that now. One deliberate refusal: the severity for `list` does NOT depend on `resourceNames`, because whether `resourceNames` narrows a list request is a question about the authorizer that could not be verified against the published reference in this session -- the conservative reading is right whichever way it falls, and resting a verdict on an unverified fact is what G229 and G231 are about. And the finding behind all of it: this work began as backlog item R1, created by reading three external reviews' gap lists instead of the code, which produced a planned rule for something that already existed. A gap list from someone who cannot run the tool is a hypothesis | `tests/test_rbac_secret_reads.py::test_list_on_secrets_outranks_get_because_it_returns_their_contents`, `tests/test_rbac_secret_reads.py::test_a_get_narrowed_to_named_secrets_is_not_an_escalation_path`, `tests/test_rbac_secret_reads.py::test_list_is_reported_whatever_resource_names_says`, `tests/test_rbac_secret_reads.py::test_a_cluster_role_outranks_a_role_for_the_same_verbs` |
| G241 | **`KN-S036`, and the image reference that does not look like one.** All three external reviews asked for a registry allowlist and the catalogue had none -- verified against the code this time, after R1 turned out to be a rule that already existed (G240). The allowlist is `audit.allowedRegistries` in the configuration file, and deliberately NOT a command-line flag or an MCP tool argument: which registries an organisation has approved is an organisational decision that should be the same for every caller of a deployment, not something a caller picks per request. With none configured the rule **declines**, which keeps it out of the evaluated list, because reporting every image as approved for want of a policy is the absence-read-as-pass failure the whole catalogue is built against. The reference parser is the content. Docker's rule is that the first path component is the registry only when it LOOKS like a host -- it contains a dot or a colon, or it is exactly `localhost` -- so `nginx` and `bitnami/nginx` both come from `docker.io` however little they look like it, and an allowlist that missed that would pass every unqualified image, which is the commonest reference there is. The finding says so in that case, because a reference that names no registry is exactly when a reader needs telling which one they are pulling from. An entry may narrow to one organisation (`quay.io/my-org`), which is the only useful form for a registry anyone can push to, and the match is on a path SEGMENT so `quay.io/my-org-evil/app` cannot impersonate it. One thing deliberately left undone and written down rather than half-built: this rule belongs in admission, and it is the only entry in `NOT_GENERATED` whose reason is "not yet" rather than "cannot" -- the generator's contract is that a policy fails exactly what the audit reports, so the allowlist has to reach `policy generate` first, and a policy emitted with an empty list would admit everything while looking like enforcement | `tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_the_registry_is_read_the_way_a_runtime_reads_it`, `tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_it_declines_rather_than_passing_everything_when_no_policy_is_configured`, `tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_an_entry_can_narrow_to_one_organisation`, `tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_a_prefix_cannot_be_impersonated_by_a_longer_name`, `tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_an_unqualified_image_is_measured_against_docker_hub_and_says_so`, `tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_an_embedded_pod_spec_is_checked_too`, `tests/test_policy_generate.py::test_audit_flags_the_bad_fixture_and_not_the_good` |
| G242 | **A generated policy is operator input compiled into an enforcing expression, so what it cannot compile it refuses to emit.** `KN-S036` was the one rule in `NOT_GENERATED` whose reason was "not yet": the allowlist now reaches `policy generate` from `audit.allowedRegistries` and the rule is generated for both engines. Three decisions are the content. **The CEL is written to agree with the audit case by case, not approximately.** Every test carries its separator, so `quay.io/my-org` admits `quay.io/my-org` itself, `quay.io/my-org/app:1` and `quay.io/my-org@sha256:...`, and refuses `quay.io/my-org:1` (the audit compares the tag as part of the path), `quay.io/my-org-evil/app` and `quay.io:443/my-org/app` -- a different registry. **Two kinds of allowlist are refused rather than compiled.** An entry naming `docker.io` needs the "is the first path component a host" test, which the API server's CEL cannot do without `split`; an entry whose host has no dot, port or `localhost` is never a registry to a runtime, so `myregistry/app` comes from Docker Hub and a `startsWith('myregistry/')` policy would ADMIT an image the audit reports. Both are stated in the file header instead, because a policy that disagrees with the audit is worse than no policy, and an unquotable string reaching an enforcing expression is worse still -- a policy that fails to compile denies every request under `failurePolicy: Fail`. **An empty list still generates nothing**, for the same reason the audit declines: enforcement that enforces nothing is the absence-read-as-a-pass failure in its most expensive form. The agreement is asserted, not asserted twice: the parity test compares the policy's verdict with the audit's over 80 reference shapes and four allowlists, and the 2,700-object random cross-check now runs with an allowlist so the rule is in it. CI's kind cluster generates it through a written config file, so the one expression built from operator-supplied strings is the one a real API server type-checks | `tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_the_generated_policy_refuses_exactly_what_the_audit_reports`, `tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_an_allowlist_the_generator_cannot_compile_emits_nothing`, `tests/test_policy_generate.py::test_registry_rule_is_generated_only_with_an_allowlist`, `tests/test_policy_generate.py::test_every_rule_is_generated_or_has_a_reason`, `tests/test_policy_generate.py::test_policy_equals_audit_on_random_objects`, `ci:Generated policies on the API server` |
| G243 | **One configured list, three readers, and every way of getting it wrong fails loudly.** `KN-S036`'s allowlist reached `kube-noob audit` (G241) and the generated policies (G242), and the bundled webhook still skipped the rule, so a cluster running the sidecar had no registry check at all. The refusals are the content. **`admission.enforce: [KN-S036]` with no allowlist is refused**, by the chart at install and by `Config.build` at startup: the rule declines without a list, so enforcement would deny nothing while the values file said a registry policy was being enforced. **An entry that could never match is refused when the configuration loads** -- an image reference is lower-case and its first component is a registry only when it has a dot, a port or is exactly `localhost`, so `MyRegistry` and `myregistry` match nothing and, left in, would report every image in the cluster as unapproved: 93 rules working and one lying is worse than a startup error. **The values schema refuses the same shapes** so the error arrives at `helm install` rather than in a crash loop, and the webhook's pod annotation includes the list so it restarts when the list changes rather than enforcing yesterday's | `tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_an_entry_that_could_never_match_is_refused_at_load`, `tests/test_registry_allowlist.py::test_the_webhook_and_the_audit_read_the_same_list`, `tests/test_admission.py::test_the_registry_rule_declines_rather_than_warning_without_an_allowlist`, `tests/test_admission.py::test_chart_carries_the_registry_allowlist_to_both_config_files`, `ci:Chart guards (fail-closed NetworkPolicy, fsGroup only off-OpenShift, web flag wired to env)` |
| G245 | **The same image, written three ways, has to get one verdict -- and the audit was the side that was wrong.** A red team of G242/G243 went looking for a case where the generated policy and the audit disagree and found the disagreement inside the audit instead: `_registry_allowed` compared an allowlist entry against the text after the first slash, so `docker.io/library` admitted `library/nginx` and reported both `nginx` and `docker.io/library/nginx`, and `docker.io/bitnami` reported `docker.io/bitnami/nginx`, the fully qualified form of exactly what it was written to allow. G243 had just turned that false report into an admission denial. The fix is to normalise the reference the way a runtime resolves it -- strip the host the reference states, and expand a single-component Docker Hub path to the `library/` organisation it lives in -- and the entry is NOT normalised with it, because an entry's path is an organisation prefix rather than a repository. | G246 | **Two views of one audit have to carry one set of numbers, and that meant deciding which of them is the audit.** `ksi` and `fedramp_ksi` each ran their own: neither took an exception list, so a signed-off finding was counted open on a compliance page, and both audited at INFO while `audit` defaults to `low`, so the same bundle produced two figures for one control. | G247 | **A guard against over-matching became a guard against matching.** A sweep of forty questions a platform engineer would type then found one more wrong answer and seven missing ones. The wrong one: "my pod is being denied by pod security admission" went to the NetworkPolicy reachability checker, because `pod security` put `pod` in the sentence twice and `\bpod\b.*\bpod\b` was all that route needed -- it refuses an admission denial now, which `policy explain` answers. The missing ones each had an exact command: the rule catalogue, whether the cluster records API requests (the G244 block), an image from an unapproved registry or on a mutable tag, watching a namespace for incidents, an OCPBUGS key, whether the audit reads the KServe and Ray CRDs, and the training bench. Every new route was then attacked with text sharing its words -- "check all pods", "image pull failed from quay", "the namespace has a watch annotation" -- and tightened until none routes; the catalogue route's phrases are written out flat because `(?:rule|check)` nested inside one makes the import guard read a bare `check` as its word, and that refusal is correct. Two defects in the question router, and they are each other's mirror. One: "which secrets are exposed in the public repo?" routed to `posture scan`, which walks Routes, ServiceAccounts and RBAC in a cluster -- the question is about files in Git. It routes to `audit` now, where `KN-S018` and `KN-S026` are, and the exposure route carries a negative lookahead for repository words, so the wrong answer is not merely ranked second behind the right one (the cap of two would have hidden it). Two: "is anything in my cluster exposed to the internet?" -- the plainest form of the question `posture scan` exists for -- matched NOTHING, because `internet` and `public` sat in the route's first word group, and `_refuse_a_word_in_two_groups` forbids the second group from holding them too, so no sentence could satisfy both groups with those words alone. The fix is phrases in the second group (`internet-facing`, `publicly`, `to the internet`, `from outside`, `outside the cluster`) rather than the bare nouns, which also keeps a log line reading "no route to host" and a namespace called `public` out of it. The lesson is the one G237 half-learned: a structural guard changes what the table can express, so every guard needs a case asserting what it must still ALLOW -- not only what it must refuse. `_route` now accepts a leading negative lookahead, which is what a route needs to say what it is not about, and the anchoring invariant is unchanged because `\\A(?!.*word)` scans forward once like any other lookahead | `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_question_about_a_repository_is_answered_by_the_audit_first`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_question_reaches_the_command_that_answers_it`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_nothing_is_routed_for_text_that_only_shares_words`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_a_negative_lookahead_may_lead_a_route_and_is_still_anchored`, `tests/test_capability_routes.py::test_every_route_is_anchored_so_the_cost_stays_linear` |

| G249 | **The API type's comment is the contract; the operator's manifests are the behaviour, and they had diverged.** G244 built the detection-coverage statement from the field documentation of `Audit` in openshift/api, which says `Default` is metadata "with the exception of events (not logged at all)". Two details decide whether that is useful or merely noisy, and each is the opposite defect. **The owner check does not apply**: a Pod owned by a ReplicaSet is normally skipped because its owner was audited and carries the same spec, but no owner carries an ephemeral container, so skipping it would have left every managed workload's Pod -- which is most of them -- as the one request nothing checks. **Only what the request adds is reported**: the webhook already compared findings with the old object to decide a denial, and the same comparison now filters the warnings, so a `kubectl debug` on a Pod that already breaks an enforced rule is still allowed and the one line that matters is not buried under the workload's whole report.
