| G251 | **A line number that is not a line of any file somebody can open is a wrong answer, not a vague one.** The audit reads one string, so `-f dir/` concatenates the directory's files with `---` between them -- and every finding after the first file was reported at a line of that concatenation, which for a three-file directory was commonly past the end of the file the finding was in, with no path to say which file that was. `_dir_yaml`'s own docstring had already made this argument for an unreadable file: it deserved "a path anyone could open" rather than "a line of the combined text". The findings deserved the same. The input pipeline now records where each file starts in the combined text -- counted off the text that precedes it, separator included, because whether a file ends with a newline changes the arithmetic and a number that is right for most inputs is the defect being replaced -- and each finding is put back in its own file before anything is printed, emitted, compacted or turned into OSCAL. Two deliberate omissions: piped input names no file, because there is nothing to name, and the OSCAL document carries the file but not the line, because the line depends on where a document sits inside its file and that document is held to being identical for a reordered bundle. The same reading of the input pipeline found a second trap in it: `fix -f dir/` reads several files and writes ONE stream, so a caller redirecting it over their directory would have one file where they had five. It is not refused -- the output is correct YAML and somebody may want exactly that -- but it says so on stderr, where the change summary already goes | `tests/test_finding_locations.py::test_every_finding_names_its_own_file_and_that_files_line`, `tests/test_finding_locations.py::test_one_file_is_named_too_and_its_lines_are_unchanged`, `tests/test_finding_locations.py::test_stdin_claims_no_file`, `tests/test_finding_locations.py::test_the_text_report_prints_the_path_and_the_line`, `tests/test_finding_locations.py::test_oscal_names_the_file_and_deliberately_not_the_line`, `tests/test_finding_locations.py::test_an_excepted_finding_is_located_too`, `tests/test_oscal.py::test_the_document_does_not_depend_on_the_order_the_documents_arrived_in`, `tests/test_finding_locations.py::test_fix_says_it_merged_a_directory` |
| G252 | **A guard that cannot see a word is a guard that approves it, and a fact that holds for one cluster shape is not a fact.** A third red team, and five classes. **A crash is a verdict**: two `APIServer` objects, one with `metadata: hello` -- truthy, so `.get` on a string -- raised out of the detection block and `kube-noob audit` exited 1 with a traceback, and exit 1 means findings at or above `--fail-on`. **Hosted control planes are a different cluster**: HyperShift generates a hosted kube-apiserver's policy from its OWN vendored library-go, which still dropped core Events at release-4.18 and dropped the rule at release-4.19, so ROSA HCP at 4.18 and standalone OpenShift at 4.18 give opposite answers for the same object and profile. **A version is not a number**: `--version 1.33` is a documented input for an OpenShift target and was compared against `(4, 18)`, deciding the row from a comparison with no meaning; it is mapped through the release table now, and a major this build has never seen is undecidable rather than "later". **A manifest's typo is part of the behaviour**: the `machineconfiguration.openshift.io` exclusion arrived in 4.18 and spells its key `resource` rather than `resources`, which library-go's non-strict loader drops, so the rule covers every resource of the group -- the row says so, and says it only from 4.18. **The canonical form is not a defect**: KServe's own sample predictor has no `image`, because the ServingRuntime supplies it, and the catalogue called that a HIGH finding -- which G248 then turned into an admission denial of the upstream sample. It is an assumption in the report now, not a finding. And the router: G247's negative lookaheads silenced real questions, which is the defect G247 itself was fixing in the other direction, so the repository guard is gone (table order does that work) and the admission guard is a denial phrase rather than the bare nouns. The meta-fix is the guard: `_literal_words` expands `rules?` into `rule` and `rules` and `reach(?:es|ed|able)?` into all four, and a word CONTAINED in another group's phrase is refused -- which is what let `every rule` satisfy both halves of a route written to need two things. The last finding was a cost, and a cost is a bypass: the locator scanned every sibling of a list for every finding, so a Deployment padded with 2,000 containers took 6 seconds and one with 16,000 took SIX MINUTES -- past the webhook's three-second timeout, so the API server admitted the object with no warning and no denial, and the single process stayed busy while every other request timed out with it. | G253 | **A substring test against a dotted literal is how URL-validation bugs are written, so a scanner is right to flag one wherever it appears.** CodeQL's `py/incomplete-url-substring-sanitization` fired on four assertions in this PR -- `"docker.io" in message`, `"example.com" in assumption` and two more -- none of which validates a URL: they check that a FINDING says what it should. The alert is still worth acting on rather than suppressing, for two reasons. A reviewer cannot tell a real one from a false one at a glance, so a repository that carries four false ones has taught its reviewers to skim the fifth. And the shape it objects to is a weak assertion anyway: `"docker.io" in message` passes on a message that names the registry anywhere, including in a sentence that says the opposite. Every one of them is a phrase match now -- `pulls from 'docker\.io'`, `allowedRegistries \(quay\.io/my-org, registry\.example\.com\)`, `API group example\.com\b.*not audited` -- which asserts the sentence the rule actually produces, with the dots escaped, and leaves nothing for the scanner to read as a hostname check. | G254 | **A chart is a claim nobody checks the arithmetic on, so it may only restate one.** The HTML report (U1) grew charts, and every rule about them follows from that sentence. **A chart shows only what a table on the same page shows**: the severity bars and the trend each ship with the table they are drawn from, which is why adding them added two tables the page did not have. **Colour is never the only carrier** -- every bar is labelled with its name and its count beside the mark, because the page is read in print, in forced colours and by someone with no colour vision; the palettes were run through a colour-vision validator (lightness band, chroma floor, adjacent-pair separation, contrast against the surface) in both light and dark, which is how the amber `excepted` bucket became teal: next to the red `findings` bucket it failed the separation check that the two buckets a reader must never confuse are exactly the ones to get right. **And an absence is never drawn as a zero** -- an empty bar and a missing bar are the same picture, so the trend is withheld entirely when any snapshot in the series could not read every resource, which is the rule `posture report` already applies to control standing for the same reason: a line through a floor publishes a fall in findings that may only be a fall in what kube-noob was allowed to see. The geometry is integer arithmetic over the counts, so the tests assert that twice the findings is twice the bar instead of looking at a picture, and the escaping rule of the page applies one layer down, inside the SVG. The red team of the first version found the rule's sharpest case: every report written before 0.8 carries no `notRead` key, under the SAME format string, so reading a missing key as "nothing was unread" marked a folder inherited across an upgrade as fourteen complete reads. | G255 | **Two different things can be unseen, and a record of one is not a record of the other.** A posture snapshot recorded `notRead` -- what the cluster refused -- and dropped the audit's `assumptions`, which is where "N pod spec(s) are in kinds kube-noob has no reader for, NOT audited" lives. | G256 | **Generating three sentences does not guard a document; it guards three sentences.** G206 generated `docs/compliance.md` and P8 generated the limits table, the compaction measurement and the roadmap's measured lines -- and "the same 92 rules" sat in a published document beside code that had 93, because nothing had been said about the numbers nobody generated. Every countable claim a reader can check is either generated or paired with the thing that measures it, and a guard whose sentence has been reworded FAILS rather than silently stops guarding: a claim that leaves the guard without anyone noticing is the same defect one step earlier. The guard's own first version is the cautionary half: it intersected indicator control ids with kube-noob's by hand, missed that one side is lower case, and produced "46 of 46 indicators have no kube-noob rule behind them" -- arithmetic that was wrong by a factor of three and would have read as authoritative in prose. | G257 | **A summary that disagrees with the table under it is worse than no summary.** U5 put the verdict in the largest type on the page, and the first version built it by summing the per-control standing column -- which counts one finding once per control it is evidence about, so the headline said 5 where the report said 3. A number in a masthead is quoted into tickets and read by people who scroll no further, so it is taken from the same field the table prints, never re-derived from a different aggregate; where a count really is per-control, the unit says "rule results, counted once per control" rather than "rules". | G258 | **"Does not do the old thing" is not "does the new thing", and a renamed field is a wrong answer rather than a refused one.** P4 read v1alpha2 ClusterNetworkPolicy, and the whole risk was that the kind LOOKS like the v1alpha1 pair: feed a v1alpha2 rule to the older evaluator and `protocols` goes unrecognised, which its port matcher reads as "every port" -- a rule scoped to TCP 9090 silently widened to every port, in the direction that produces a confident wrong allow. A renamed or restructured field is never read by the nearest older shape; it gets a reader that knows the new one, or the document is refused by name. The same rule in the CNI table: Calico 3.32 dropping AdminNetworkPolicy and Calico 3.32 enforcing ClusterNetworkPolicy are two sourced facts, and deriving one from the other would read every CNI that enforces neither as enforcing the new kind. And when a kind stops being unread, whatever was compensating for not reading it has to be re-examined -- and re-examined rather than deleted. Evaluating this one removed the caveat that warned a NetworkPolicy could not override a cluster-scoped Deny, and the proposal it was protecting immediately became wrong; and the first attempt at tidying the now-empty list of unread cluster-scoped kinds nearly took the undetermined verdict away from the two CNI kinds that are still unread, which was the wrong half to empty -- the protection keys off the class, not off whichever kind happened to be unread when it was written. A red-team pass over the finished module found nine more of the same shape, every one a wrong ALLOW: the second reading built to withhold an unenforced tier cleared the OLD tiers and not the new ones, so the two readings always agreed and nothing was ever withheld; a document the reader refused became a clean answer instead of the undetermined one it produced before the kind was readable; a named port resolved by number alone accepted TCP on a port declared UDP; a Baseline deny took the Admin tier's "no NetworkPolicy can override this" fix, which the spec contradicts in as many words; and shapes the CRD refuses -- `protocols: []`, `tcp: {}`, an empty `from`, an invalid selector -- were read as "every port" or "matches nothing" rather than as unreadable. The lesson under all of them: making something readable moves it from one code path to another, and every guard, every second reading and every default on the OLD path has to be re-checked against the new one | `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_a_policy_whose_enforcement_is_unknown_is_withheld_rather_than_believed`, `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_a_policy_that_cannot_be_evaluated_makes_the_answer_undetermined`, `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_a_named_port_is_matched_on_its_own_protocol`, `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_a_baseline_deny_is_fixable_with_a_networkpolicy_and_an_admin_deny_is_not`, `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_a_protocols_shape_the_api_refuses_is_not_read_as_every_port`, `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_a_rule_with_no_peers_is_not_read_as_matching_nothing`, `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_a_selector_the_api_refuses_does_not_crash_and_does_not_vanish`, `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_an_uncheckable_peer_is_not_described_as_an_unrecognised_field` | `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_protocols_scope_a_rule_to_its_ports`, `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_accept_is_the_action_and_allow_is_not`, `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_an_unrecognised_peer_fails_closed_on_a_deny_rule`, `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_an_unrecognised_peer_on_an_accept_rule_matches_nothing`, `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_two_baseline_policies_at_one_priority_are_undetermined`, `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_not_enforcing_the_old_api_is_not_the_same_claim_as_enforcing_the_new_one`, `tests/test_netpol_cnp_guard.py::test_the_fix_for_a_cluster_scoped_deny_is_not_a_networkpolicy`, `tests/test_netpol_cnp.py::test_making_one_kind_readable_did_not_retire_the_guard_for_the_others` |
| G259 | **A page three of four commands can produce is not a feature, and one wording over several report shapes is a wrong number waiting for its turn.** U5 rebuilt the top of the HTML report from each report's own keys and was unit-tested against reports built by hand -- so nobody ever ran `ksi --html`, where an indicator row's `findings` is an integer count rather than the bucket dict a posture report carries. The masthead read it as a dict, raised `AttributeError`, and the command wrote no file at all: a crash in the one artifact that leaves the terminal, on a path every unit test agreed was fine. Two rules come out of it. A surface that several commands render is tested by RUNNING each of those commands end to end, because a hand-built fixture is a guess about the shape and the shape is the defect; and a reader that meets a type it does not recognise produces a number it can defend or says nothing, never an exception and never a confident zero. The same pass found the wording defect underneath: one sentence served all three report shapes, so the crosswalk page announced "21 findings are open, and 15 rules reached no verdict" over tiles reading "indicators" and sent the reader to a section 1 it does not have -- G257's own rule broken in the page that introduced G257. The sentence is built in the same branch as the numbers it describes, in that shape's own units, naming only a section that shape renders | `tests/test_report_html.py::test_every_command_that_writes_html_actually_writes_it`, `tests/test_report_html.py::test_the_ksi_masthead_survives_a_findings_count_of_any_shape`, `tests/test_report_html.py::test_the_ksi_masthead_counts_indicators_and_claims_no_section_it_lacks`, `tests/test_report_html.py::test_the_verdict_agrees_with_the_table_it_summarises`, `tests/test_report_html.py::test_the_masthead_never_points_at_a_section_the_page_does_not_have` |
| G260 | **A test guarded against one absence and not the other decides by accident which one the environment has.** `ci/test (3.13)` was red while 3.11, 3.12 and 3.14 were green, and the Python version had nothing to do with it. `tests/test_video_loudness.py` guards the module on ffmpeg and ffprobe, but one test also did a bare `import audio_kit`, which needs numpy -- and numpy is in no dev extra, because the videos job installs it alongside ffmpeg and runs this file itself. So on a runner without ffmpeg the module skipped and the import never happened; on a runner with ffmpeg it ran and raised `ModuleNotFoundError`. The verdict was decided by which tools the runner happened to carry, which is the same defect this project exists to refuse, turned on its own suite: a missing thing read as a pass three times out of four, and as a failure the fourth. Reproduced on a fresh 3.12 env as readily as on 3.13, which killed the version theory. The import is `pytest.importorskip` now, the idiom `tests/test_video_voice.py` already used for the same module -- so where numpy is present the test runs and where it is absent it says so, instead of depending on an unrelated binary. The rule: a test's guard covers every absence that test needs, not the first one somebody noticed | `tests/test_video_loudness.py::test_mux_keeps_the_whole_picture`, `ci:videos` |
| G261 | **A guard that cannot pass is not a guard, and a flag bundle in quotes is one argument.** `ci/helm-and-dogfood` held PR #47 red, and the step was the registry-allowlist guard G243 added -- the one that proves `audit.allowedRegistries` reaches both config files, the sidecar's and the webhook's, so `kube-noob audit` and the admission webhook give one verdict. It was written `reg="--set audit.allowedRegistries={...}"` and used `"$reg"`. Quoted, that is a SINGLE argv element, so helm answered `unknown flag: --set audit.allowedRegistries`, the pipe fed nothing to python, and `yaml.safe_load` returned `None`. The variable was designed to be word-split like its neighbour `$base`, which is unquoted and works. So this guard never passed once since the day it was written, and the property it exists to prove was never actually checked in CI -- the failure was at least loud, which is the only reason it was not worse. Two rules. A flag bundle is a bash array and is expanded `"${reg[@]}"`, never a string in quotes; and because that mistake is invisible on review, `tests/test_workflow_honesty.py` now scans every workflow for it, joining backslash continuations first so that a quoted element of a `for args in "$a" "$b"` list -- which IS correct, since `cmd $args` splits it later -- is not flagged. The guard was written before the fix and named both real sites and neither false positive | `tests/test_workflow_honesty.py::test_no_bundle_of_flags_is_passed_to_a_command_as_a_single_argument` |
| G262 | A corpus shard bigger than the public bundle allows is stored as parts, not by raising the cap. `write_shard` keeps each file at or under `PART_MAX` (250 MB), which is under `db_bundle.MAX_MEMBER` (300 MB). The parts are `<shard>.jsonl` then `<shard>-2.jsonl`. `shard_paths` reads them in order from the manifest, so a leftover file from a larger write is not part of the corpus. A rewrite that fits in one file removes the extra parts | `tests/test_db_bundle.py::test_a_shard_over_the_member_cap_is_written_as_parts_and_read_back` |
| G263 | **A wall-clock budget is a proxy for a property, and it is the proxy that depends on the machine.** `test_a_padded_object_does_not_outrun_the_webhooks_timeout` asserted `elapsed < 5.0` for 4,000 containers. It held for months, then measured 5.44 s on one shared 3.11 runner and failed -- on the 0.9.0 release commit, while 3.12, 3.13 and 3.14 passed the same code. Locally that object costs 0.86 s, so the budget had no headroom on a slow runner and the verdict was about the machine, not the code. The test is named for a shape, and its own docstring says the fix "makes the cost linear": quadratic in four times the input is about sixteen times the time, linear about four. So it measures 1,000 and 4,000 back to back on the same machine and asserts the ratio stays under 8 -- a slow runner slows both halves and the ratio holds. Proved rather than assumed: with G252's memo disabled, the ratio is 15.7x (1,000 = 2.01 s, 4,000 = 31.46 s) and the new assertion fires, so it catches the regression the budget caught while no longer catching the runner. A warm-up call comes first, because the cold call pays for imports -- 500 containers cost 1.33 s cold against 0.2 s warm, which would make the small sample carry the startup and the ratio read as sub-linear. `test_the_memory_demo_runs_offline_under_a_second` had the same shape (1.32 s against a 1.0 s budget, passing 3/3 when idle) and its ceiling is now loose enough that only a hang trips it, with the contract left to the assertions on what the demo printed. The rule: assert the invariant, and give a clock only a ceiling a catastrophe would have to cross | `tests/test_admission.py::test_a_padded_object_does_not_outrun_the_webhooks_timeout`, `tests/test_memory_repo.py::test_the_memory_demo_runs_offline_under_a_second` |
| G264 | **The gate everyone is told to run claimed to run every gate CI runs, and ran eleven fewer.** `scripts/check.sh` is what AGENTS.md tells an agent to run after any change to `src/`, and its own header said it ran "every gate CI runs on code". It ran `pytest -q` where CI runs `pytest --cov --cov-fail-under=80`, so the coverage floor was never enforced locally, and it never ran `python -m build`, the wheel smoke test, `e2e_mcp_offline.py`, `check_publishable.sh`, `uv lock --check`, either `pip-audit`, `e2e_plugin.py`, `brew_formula.py --check`, or the OSCAL validation and its did-it-skip guard. It then passed four times running while CI failed: `test (3.13)` on a missing numpy (G260), `helm-and-dogfood` on a flag bundle in quotes (G261), and twice on main at the 0.9.0 release commit (G263). A gate that passes while the real gate fails is an absence reading as a pass -- this project's own thesis, aimed at its own gate. It runs all of those now, and a tool it cannot find is collected and printed under NOT RUN with the words "this is not a pass", rather than passed over in silence; `helm` is the only entry on this machine. `tests/test_check_sh.py` reads ci.yml and fails if a gate a code job runs is neither in check.sh nor named in NOT_LOCAL with a reason -- and it earned its keep immediately: its first version matched only `scripts/*` paths, so it missed the plugin's `uv export \| pip-audit`, which check.sh had also missed. The hole and the guard had the same blind spot, which is the argument for writing the guard against ci.yml rather than against a list | `tests/test_check_sh.py::test_every_gate_a_ci_code_job_runs_is_run_by_check_sh`, `tests/test_check_sh.py::test_check_sh_enforces_the_same_coverage_floor_as_ci`, `tests/test_check_sh.py::test_a_missing_tool_is_recorded_rather_than_passed_over`, `tests/test_check_sh.py::test_check_sh_does_not_claim_to_run_what_it_cannot` |
| G265 | **A check that runs before the thing it checks is not a check, and `dist/` is the publish action's whole argument.** The pinned `pypa/gh-action-pypi-publish` runs `twine check dist/*` and then `exec twine upload dist/*` over the WHOLE directory. twine 7.0.0 rejects anything that is not a Python distribution -- `sbom.spdx.json` with "Unknown distribution format", the krew archive with "No PKG-INFO in archive" -- and aborts before it opens a connection. From cf08981 (2026-11-05) both were copied into `dist/`, purely so `github-release` would attach them. v0.7.1 shipped before that; v0.8.0 died earlier on a missing PyYAML; v0.9.0 died one step earlier still on a Rekor 404. So `publish-pypi` had never once run with those files present, and the first release that got past attestation would have failed with nothing published. Two blind spots hid it. `scripts/check_publishable.sh` is the deliberate twin of the action's own check -- same action SHA, same Python, same bundled twine -- and it ran BEFORE the two steps that contaminated `dist/`, so it was checking a directory that no longer existed by the time the action read it. And ci.yml's `publishable` job builds the dists without ever running the SBOM or krew steps, so no pull request could see it either: the sequence existed only in release.yml and was first executed on a tag. Non-distribution release assets live in `assets/` now, `check_publishable.sh` runs last over the `dist/` the action will actually see, and three tests hold it: nothing but a distribution is written into `dist/`, the publishable check is ordered after every step that touches it, and provenance is attempted twice while still deciding the job. Reproduced locally with the action's exact twine before changing anything: `twine check dist/*` exit 1, `twine upload dist/*` exit 1 | `tests/test_workflow_honesty.py::test_nothing_but_a_python_distribution_is_written_into_dist`, `tests/test_workflow_honesty.py::test_the_publishable_check_runs_after_everything_that_touches_dist`, `tests/test_workflow_honesty.py::test_provenance_is_attempted_twice_and_still_decides_the_job` |
| G267 | **Deleting the engine from a plugin leaves seven skills telling Claude to call tools that are not there.** The Claude plugin directory refused `plugin/kube-noob` on 2026-11-10 with two findings, both gated on a label this project cannot apply for: "Local MCP servers accepted only from Verified organizations" and "Files outside the allowed types accepted only from Verified organizations". That plugin is 218 files, 99 of them a refused type (94 `.py`, 3 `.tmpl`, `py.typed`, `pyproject.toml`, `uv.lock`, one `.svg`), plus a `.mcp.json` declaring a local stdio server, so no edit keeps the server and passes. Anthropic's own docs say there is no application for Verified status and the portal has no step for it, so waiting is not a plan. The obvious move -- strip the server out of the existing plugin -- is the trap: all seven skills were written to call `kubenoob_*` tools, 46 of them, and copying them into a plugin with no server ships seven skills whose every instruction names a tool that does not exist. That is an absence reading as a pass, this project's own thesis aimed at its own listing. So the directory gets a SECOND plugin, `plugin/kube-noob-skills`, whose skills were rewritten to carry the facts, to say which step is reasoning rather than verification, and to end with what the skill cannot do and the CLI that can. Tests hold all of it, and one earned its keep before the branch existed: five of the `kube-noob` command lines first written into those skills do not exist (`posture -f`, `rhacs --policies`, `policy generate -f`, `helm-audit --chart`, a krew install for a plugin not in the index), so every command in a fenced block is now parsed against the real argparse tree and a flag the CLI does not define fails the suite. A skill whose remedy does not run is the same failure one step later. `claude plugin validate` passes BOTH plugins, the refused one included -- it says itself that it runs "a subset" of the directory's checks -- so the official local tool cannot catch this class and these tests are the only thing that does | `tests/test_directory_plugin.py::test_no_skill_tells_claude_to_call_a_tool_that_is_not_there`, `tests/test_directory_plugin.py::test_every_file_is_a_type_the_directory_accepts`, `tests/test_directory_plugin.py::test_every_command_a_skill_hands_the_user_is_one_the_cli_accepts`, `tests/test_directory_plugin.py::test_every_skill_in_the_full_plugin_has_a_counterpart_here` |
| G268 | **The one artifact that leaves for someone else's pipeline named a schema nothing here had ever read.** `gate --sarif` writes `"$schema": "https://json.schemastore.org/sarif-2.1.0.json"` into every file it hands to GitHub code scanning, and no test, no gate and no CI step had ever checked a single document against it -- in a tool whose argument is that it works with no network. The document turns out to be valid, which is the good case and not the point: it was valid by luck, and the first field added to it would have been checked by whoever consumed it, in their pipeline, not here. The schema is now vendored from OASIS, the committee that owns SARIF, rather than from schemastore, which republishes it and is not reachable from a locked-down build; `python scripts/vendor_schemas.py --sarif` refetches it with its upstream sha256 beside it, which is more than the OSCAL schema next to it can say. The URI in the output is unchanged, because code-scanning consumers recognise it and editing a published contract to match a test is the wrong way round. Two things had to be got right to make the check mean anything: SARIF 2.1.0 declares **draft-04** and uses the draft-04 `id` keyword, which ajv 8 refuses outright (`NOT SUPPORTED: keyword "id"`), so the validator picks ajv-draft-04 on the dialect the schema declares rather than rewriting the schema to a draft the committee did not publish -- and a test asserts the dialect, so a re-vendor cannot silently fall through to the other compiler; and a second test mutates one `level` to `severe` and requires the validator to refuse it, because a validator that compiles a schema to nothing and waves everything through passes exactly as quietly as one that works. `oscal_validate.mjs` became `json_schema_validate.mjs`: one validator, two dialects, rather than a second copy of thirty lines | `tests/test_gate_sarif.py::test_the_gate_writes_sarif_that_validates_against_the_oasis_schema`, `tests/test_gate_sarif.py::test_the_validator_refuses_a_document_the_schema_does_not_allow`, `tests/test_gate_sarif.py::test_the_schema_is_the_draft_the_validator_branches_on`, `tests/test_gate_sarif.py::test_the_schema_is_vendored_with_its_provenance` |
| G269 | **A SARIF file can only say "nothing found" by being short, so the audit's caveats have to travel in it.** `audit --format sarif` finishes I2. The format is a list of results, and an audit that was handed one Pod from a namespace declines its cross-object rules, and one that meets a file it cannot parse skips it; both produce a shorter list and nothing else, which whatever reads the file next takes for a cleaner cluster. The JSON report says so in `assumptions` and `unreadable`, and a format flag that REPLACES the report would have left both behind. They go into `invocations[].toolExecutionNotifications` instead -- warnings for what was not evaluated, errors for what was not read -- with `executionSuccessful` still true, because the tool ran and what it could not decide is a notification, not a crash. Every rule, in the gate's file and the audit's, now carries the NIST SP 800-53 controls the catalogue maps it to, twice on purpose: `NIST-800-53/AC-6` tags for a code-scanning view to filter on and a `nist-800-53` list for a program to read; a rule mapped to no control has NO such property, because an empty list reads as "checked, maps to nothing". Two refusals came with it. `--format` is shared by every command, so `search --format sarif` parsed and would have printed YAML under the name: any command but `audit` now exits 2. And `--oscal --format sarif` asked for two documents and got OSCAL as YAML: also exit 2. The exit code of the audit itself is unchanged by the flag, as with `--oscal` and `--compact`. The SARIF `informationUri` also still named the account the project moved out of | `tests/test_audit_sarif.py::test_what_a_partial_audit_did_not_evaluate_is_in_the_document`, `tests/test_audit_sarif.py::test_each_rule_carries_the_controls_the_catalogue_maps_it_to`, `tests/test_audit_sarif.py::test_sarif_is_refused_for_a_command_that_has_no_findings_to_put_in_it`, `tests/test_audit_sarif.py::test_the_document_validates_against_the_oasis_schema` |
| G270 | **Which operators are installed is a question whose most dangerous answer is an empty list -- or a short one.** E2. An inventory that could not see the OLM objects and printed nothing would read exactly like a cluster with no operators, and one that dropped a file it could not parse would read exactly like a cluster without that operator; the next reader (R5 matching versions against known issues, an upgrade pre-mortem, an assessor) acts on what is missing. So there are four verdicts and only one of them lists: `listed` means nothing the answer needs was skipped, `partial` names what was, `not-determined` means no ClusterServiceVersion was read, and `no-olm` means both live reads got a 404 for `operators.coreos.com` in the exact words the backends write for a missing resource type -- not any message containing "not found", which is how an auth plugin's error once read as "not an OLM cluster". The first version was red-teamed and 21 holes were found, almost all the same one: a skipped, unreadable, truncated, foreign-kind or status-less input reading as an answer. Each is a gap now: a file in a directory that does not parse, an OLM file the must-gather reader skipped or found outside the layout `oc adm inspect` writes, an empty file, an error message or `Status` in an OLM path, a list item that is not its kind, objects past the cap, a `Subscription` of another API group (ACM, Knative), conflicting duplicates (resolved by content, so order never decides), a response with no `items`, a Subscription with no status, and a namespace with CSVs and no Subscription in a file set. The join was the other half: a Subscription joins on `installedCSV` alone, to an original CSV in its own namespace, so a copy never fabricates an install, `currentCSV` is only ever the upgrade in flight, and one CSV is one operator however many Subscriptions name it; copies fold into their original only when version and phase agree. A phase that is absent or not one OLM defines is unknown and counted as not Succeeded. Shown strings are made printable by Unicode category (`sanitize.visible`), which the must-gather analysers share. The text view prints what the JSON carries (G221), `must-gather analyze` carries the inventory in both, and `--require-complete` exits 5 on anything but `listed` or `no-olm` | | G272 | **The control plane's own trust boundaries were not read at all, and the five rules that now read them decline, with the reason, wherever the manifest does not say -- including the fifteen shapes an adversarial review found the first version reading as clean.** `KN-S037` reports an APIService with `insecureSkipTLSVerify` and a service (a missing `caBundle` is the system roots); `KN-S038` a kube-apiserver whose argv makes AlwaysAllow the authorizer (explicitly, or by giving neither `--authorization-mode` nor `--authorization-config`) or leaves anonymous authentication on (explicitly or by the default `true`), with the default and the releases it was checked at; `KN-S039` an etcd with a plaintext listener or advertised URL on a network address, or with client certificates off -- neither the switch nor a trusted CA file -- beside a network TLS listener; `KN-S040` OpenShift encryption at rest unset or `identity`; `KN-S041` an LDAP identity provider with `insecure: true` and an `ldap://` URL. Recognition is by the binary `command` runs, never a name or label; every other launch -- a shell script, a wrapper, `hyperkube apiserver`, k3s, rke2, k0s, an image entrypoint, a non-string argv -- declines with the reason, as do `$(VAR)` anywhere in argv, a flag that may swallow the next, an authorization mode the API server rejects, a `--config-file` or `envFrom`, an unknown type, an object not named `cluster` or two that are, ROSA with HCP, OpenShift's operator-managed API server, and an OpenShift object audited on another platform; `--version` and `--help` are not servers. Input with nothing of a rule's kind declines quietly, so it no longer evidences AC-14, SC-28(1) or AU-2. Each decline is one sorted line in `assumptions` naming every declined object up to a cap, a bounded `declined` map in the JSON, `notEvaluatedRules` in `controlSummary`, a bounded 'not evaluated' line in the text view and the SARIF, and is carried into the gate's JSON, SARIF, Markdown and HTML; manifest-derived text is cleaned, one line and capped. `KN-S037` is generated as a ValidatingAdmissionPolicy and not for Kyverno, whose default resourceFilters exclude APIService, and the other four say why not. Helm hooks needed no rule: a test holds that a privileged hook Job gets the findings of the same Job without the annotation. A second review's neighbours are held too: etcd `--auto-tls`/`--peer-auto-tls` with no certificate files is a finding (etcd drops client-certificate checking with its own TLS settings); a readable entrypoint kube-noob does not read -- a script file, `s6-svscan`, `/pause`, `python3 -c`, `sh <file>`, a shell after `busybox` or `env`, `mksh` -- declines when the image, a flag or a mention points at the binary, alone and beside a clean one; kube-apiserver reads `_` as `-` and etcd declines it; `--version`/`-h` count only at real flag positions; authorization combinations the API server refuses, and `KMS` without a `kms` block, decline; known booleans given bare are true; the text view's path, file name, exception lines and detection lines and the gate's annotations are cleaned; the gate's Markdown separates assumptions from declines; an exception on a declined rule is `unusedNotEvaluated`, not stale; the `audit_manifests` description and the plugins' reading guides name `declined` and `notEvaluatedRules`. A third review made recognition fail-safe: a container is not the server only when nothing -- image component, flag, `ETCD_*` variable, a command-word mention in argv, args, env or a script, an embedding binary -- points at it, or when it is positively something else (`etcdctl`, `k3s agent`, an etcdctl-and-coreutils script on the etcd image); every reproducer is held never clean alone and beside a clean neighbour. A rule that found something and declined elsewhere is in `controlSummary.partlyEvaluatedRules` (KSI, OSCAL remark, text, SARIF, gate), and `declined[rule].decided` counts the objects read in full. `command: []` is the image entrypoint; an embedded `--kube-apiserver-arg` AlwaysAllow is a finding; ABAC/Webhook decline the authorizer only; an empty `--config-file=` reads `ETCD_CONFIG_FILE`; the insecure port is CRITICAL only on a stated 1.19 or older on a non-loopback address (the two flag pairs are one variable each, 8080 by default) and declines on 1.20-1.23 (the server refuses a non-zero port), 1.24+, an assumed version or a value pflag refuses; etcd 3.6 booleans are read and `--enable-v2` declines unless the image says 3.5; kubeadm `ClusterConfiguration` and OpenShift `KubeAPIServer` are named as unread; the gate's Assumptions section is an allowlist. A fourth review: objects that write the flags (kubeadm ClusterConfiguration, Cluster API KubeadmControlPlane/KubeadmConfig and templates, the kubeadm-config ConfigMap, kind kubeadmConfigPatches, k0s ClusterConfig, Rancher provisioning Cluster) are read for AlwaysAllow, explicit anonymous-auth true and plaintext etcd URLs and otherwise named as unread; flag words and command-position absolute paths inside scripts and env, ETCD_ envFrom prefixes, quoted names and Windows .exe launches are evidence; hyperkube images by component; find/tar/sort are not safe in an etcd-image script; typed lists and PodTemplate are read; the gate HTML carries the coverage lines; KSI rows count the partly evaluated rules they do not name. A fifth review: every list whose items is a list is opened (a non-mapping item is its own document that KN-V001 reports and the control-plane rules decline), the line map is split in one pass, the document cap applies after opening for every command and the MCP server; kubeadm v1beta4 repeats sort by (name, value) before last-wins; a kubeadm form mismatching its apiVersion declines; a Rancher selector config declines naming the selector and `key+=value` is a second flag; declined config objects name skipped files and env; insecure ports outside 0-65535 and zoned addresses decline; a flag name outside the parsers' alphabet declines as refused; morePartlyEvaluated is printed where moreNotEvaluated is; the headline score is `null`/`not determined` with `summary.scoreWithheld` naming the rules while any rule declined on an object. A sixth review: the score is withheld through the one predicate `Report.score_withheld()` whenever ANY record says something was not audited -- a declined rule, a pod spec in a kind with no reader, an operator-supplied image, a refused group -- in every renderer and in the eval grader's `minScore`; `KN-V001` reports a non-string `kind` or `apiVersion`; `KN-S038`/`KN-S039` decline on any document holding an unread pod spec, a sequence of Pods as a list item included, which is named by document and line and never refused as 'no manifest'; a typed-list item with a null or empty `kind` takes the list's; a wrapper with `items`, `kind` or `apiVersion` twice keeps every object under every `items`, and `KN-V018` fires with the wrapper's line; an empty list of either spelling is exit 2 with one true message and no `KN-V001`; kubeadm v1beta4 and v1beta3 entries sort on the raw name before pflag folds `_`, an apiVersion other than v1beta3/v1beta4 and a non-string value decline, and kubeadm's own kinds get no `KN-V001`; k3s/RKE2 `--kube-apiserver-arg` is read by one function in both readers, as GetArgs does (one `--` trimmed, `-=` and `+=` a second flag, a bare key `true`); pflag trims an IP value; a flag name of a shape no server defines declines the container; Cluster API `files` and pre/postKubeadmCommands are named on every kubeadm kind; a Rancher selector given as a mapping is read, and an empty `matchLabels` selects every machine. A seventh review: undetermined is exit 1 -- `Report.undetermined()` (declined rules or `notAudited` objects) withholds the score, exits `audit` 1 whatever `--fail-on` says, makes the gate `incomplete`/not passed with the headline 'gate incomplete' in JSON, Markdown, HTML and SARIF, reaches the MCP tools, and marks every finding-derived eval check not evaluated; `notAudited` and `notEvaluated` are in the JSON; a List of empty typed lists, `items: null` and a bare `items:` are 'held no items' (exit 2), never a score or a resource; `items` twice with a non-sequence occurrence keeps every Pod under the sequence ones and KN-V018 names the other, no wrapper gets a `metadata.name` finding, a nested wrapper's record names the wrapper that holds the key, and `fix` refuses any input with a key twice (exit 1, nothing written); `fix --format json` prints JSON; the k3s argv and the Rancher config share one decision block over `--kube-apiserver-arg` values in GetArgs's key order (a padded name, a mode the API server rejects and a non-boolean `--anonymous-auth` decline as a server that does not start); kubeadm 1.36 keeps the written order and a `mergeMethod` declines; a non-string `name`, an unknown field and a key twice in a v1beta4 entry decline, a missing value is `--name=`; `$(NAME)` expands from a literal env value as the kubelet does and `$$` is a literal `$`; a selector naming the control-plane role is read; a typed-list item of another kind inherits no apiVersion; a null or non-string kind is a fixed phrase, never a repr; the HTML 'Assumed' label is an | G273 | **A blast radius is only as honest as the legs it admits it could not trace -- and the first version of this one was not.** `posture reach` (V3) starts at one workload and answers five legs, each `reached`, `none` or `not determined`: how it is exposed, the ServiceAccount token its pods hold (automounted, or projected by a volume -- `automountServiceAccountToken: false` does not stop the second), the bindings that name the account (directly, or through `system:serviceaccounts`, `system:serviceaccounts:<ns>`, `system:authenticated` or the `system:serviceaccount:` User) and what each allows, the Secrets it may read by RBAC or reach by creating a pod (names and scopes only), and the node when the pod spec can escape to it. **One view, one implementation:** every edge comes from the attack-path scan's own reach and carries the evidence line `posture scan` prints for that step plus the object and field that prove it; the inventory is put in one canonical order inside the scan, so scan and reach name the same proof and the scan is byte-stable under shuffled input (it was not, on `main` either). The object named is scored on its own spec, whatever its kind -- a Pod because it is what runs, an old ReplicaSet or a CronJob's Job because it can differ from its owner's template -- with its owner chain and what differs from the top owner (account, node escape, the differing images, the labels) as context; two different objects with the anchor's name are an error rather than a pick, an ambiguous owner a note. **An adversarial review of the first version found seventeen defects before merge**, the worst a truncated graph that cut a privileged cluster-admin pod's capabilities and then summarised the remainder as "reaches no capability". The rules that now hold, each with a test that failed on that version: the summary comes from the whole graph and truncation keeps the strongest capabilities, the node escape and the gaps first, a binding only where something kept needs it; the document is bounded in bytes as well as count (96 KB for an MCP caller, diagram included) and says what it withheld; **`not determined` is never written `none`** -- a ServiceAccount, Role or aggregated ClusterRole the input does not carry, a stand-in for `admin`, `edit` or `view` (they aggregate `aggregate-to-*` ClusterRoles, which are applied when the bundle has them, through chains, whatever the label names), a duplicated name, a `rules`, `subjects` or `verbs` that is not a list, a bound resource and verb kube-noob does not classify (rule by rule, pair by pair, counted and named), a projected token for another audience, and a refused or namespace-limited live read each become a node with the reason; **files are never complete**, because a set of files cannot show the bindings or Services it leaves out, so `none` is said of a file only where the pod spec decides it, unless `--whole-bundle` asserts the files are everything that applies, and `--require-complete` follows that; the node leg reads the audit's own dangerous-capability list, an unmasked `/proc` and Windows HostProcess, its `none` names what it checked, and hostPort is deliberately not an escape (it is an exposure); every text field is one line and every Mermaid label escaped, so a newline or a quote in a name forges neither a row nor a `click` statement, and the limitations are printed in text and drawn as note nodes. **The scan is corrected in the same primitives:** it now ranks the escalation rights the Kubernetes RBAC good-practices and authorization pages name -- TokenRequest, Secret creation with a way to use the token, exec by `get`, ephemeral containers, PersistentVolume creation, webhook control, CSR creation with approval -- each pinned to its page in `attack_paths._api_capabilities`; a cross-namespace RoleBinding through a group is no longer skipped and its path names that namespace; measured on every fixture, `seeded.yaml` gains one score-5 path and nothing else moves. **A second review found that six fixes had moved the defect, and nine more defects (N1-N12, R1-R3); each now has a failing-first test and the neighbour of each was tried.** RBAC matches as the API server matches (`ResourceMatches`: `*`, the name, `*/<sub>`), each rule is classified resource by verb, and harmless is a named list with a source per entry (reviews, token reviews, leases, events, status writes, role writes without `bind`/`escalate`, OpenShift scope impersonation, a TokenRequest for itself) -- 8 of 10 legs determined on a realistic app, operator and bootstrap set, from 6; `secrets create` needs a way to use the token; full control does not list the rights inside it again, so cluster-admin keeps `main`'s six paths. Gateway API HTTPRoutes and Istio VirtualServices are exposures and are on the live allowlist and the chart's read Role; other routing kinds, a front to a Service not in the files, an RBAC object not read, an unreadable subject, a refused SCC list for a pod creator, a token Secret at the token path not in the files, and `containers` that is not a list are not determined; hostPort and hostNetwork are exposure. Legacy `authorization.openshift.io` RBAC is read as the objects it proxies. Duplicates: Roles not read, Services each followed and named, workloads each scored and placed apart, the same object twice counted once. Every list in the document is bounded, open legs first, and no input exceeds the byte cap. The scan diff against `main` over every fixture and 112 stress files is classified in the CHANGELOG; KN-S010 shares the scan's node-takeover list. **A third review found ten more.** A failed live list -- 404 included, since an Istio before 1.22 or a Gateway API before v1.0 answers it for the version read, and kube-noob reads no discovery -- is never an absence (`notServed` removed; 18 of 25 legs determined live on plain Kubernetes, from 22); `--read-namespace` leaves exposure open to routes from namespaces not read, and unmodelled routes count at their backends; same-name workloads are kept (a copy's cluster-admin token was dropped for the other's hostPID); token Secrets by projection, env or envFrom; VirtualService delegates and mirrors; route header values masked in `cluster_get`; the summary follows the exposure leg; reach names the scan's duplicates; malformed exposure fields are unknowns; server-set fields do not make two objects. KN-S010's critical set is the credential and API paths only (`/etc/kubernetes`, `/var/lib/kubelet/pki` and `/pods`, `/root`, `/var/lib/etcd` and below, runtime sockets anywhere), not every path under `/etc` or `/run`, which made `gate --fail-on critical` fail on kube-proxy and every CNI; the exit-code changes are listed in the CHANGELOG. **A fourth review found ten more.** Objects without a namespace go where `-n` puts them, as `kubectl apply -n` does, and without it their account legs are not determined (they were placed in `default`, and Argo CD's controller read "read Secrets (default)"); same-name copies are placed apart, never merged (the round-three union joined one copy's vulnerable image to the other's cluster-admin token: 58.8 where main said 29.4), with `ambiguous` in rank, gate and assess output; HTTPRoute mirrors, GRPCRoutes (read live too), non-Service backends, selectorless and ExternalName Services and bad Service types are modelled or not determined; live exposure is never `none`; route query parameters, Route header actions and Ingress credential snippets are masked; KN-S010 adds runtime socket directories, their ancestors and `/proc/<pid>/root`, which makes `/run` and `/var/run` critical. **A fifth review found six more.** Unplaced fronts and bindings open exposure and RBAC (the workload and its account were the only ones checked); copies are `ref#n` everywhere and counted once each, with the differing fields named; `-n` reaches vuln report, code report, gate and the MCP tools; more credential carriers are masked; KN-S010 carves `/etc/kubernetes/node-feature-discovery` and `/etc/kubernetes/secrets-store-csi-providers` out of the tree and keeps readOnly mounts critical | | G274 | **An alert becomes an evidence report the server can keep, the time it took is measured, and nothing a sender writes can stretch it, sink it or steer it (I1).** `POST /v1/alert-reports` takes Alertmanager's own payload and returns one report per alert, each section made by the `diagnose` tool called with that alert alone (signals, playbook diagnosis, reported issues for the release that alert's own label names, approved team memory, read-only next steps), so the report cannot drift from what the tools answer. Every section states what it could not determine: a release outside the local issues, or a memory repository that could not be read, is `not-evaluated`, never an empty match. An alert with no recognised signal is kept and never called healthy; a resolved alert is recorded, not diagnosed; an alert whose diagnosis failed or that the time budget (`alertReports.budgetSeconds`) did not reach keeps a `not-evaluated` row and is named; items that are not alerts and alerts past 500 are counted. A size cap keeps a row for every alert and takes detail from resolved and low-severity alerts first, naming them; the summary is computed before it. Names in suggested commands come only from the alert's own labels and only when they are valid Kubernetes names, in the report and in plain `diagnose` of a payload. Values are redacted before they are clipped and lose their `scheme://` URLs; the shared redactor's flag pattern runs only near a keyword (same output, linear time), and the body is read, built and rendered off the event loop, two reports at a time. The HTTP server alone writes reports, and only with `alertReports.store` (the chart refuses it with more than one replica): owner-only, atomic, never through a link, bounded by count, age and size; retention never removes the report just written and counts what it cannot remove; ids are checked against `[0-9]` and `\Z`; read paths change nothing; a stored file is type-checked when read back and a damaged one is a 500. Storage off or unwritable is `stored: false` with the reason. The safety contract fails on any boolean that defaults to on without a reviewed line. `/v1/reports` stays the posture report. Second review: a post waits at most 5 s for one of two report slots, shared with the MCP tool, and is then answered 503 with `Retry-After` (an MCP call gets a busy tool error); the budget counts from receipt; a post whose sender has gone is neither built nor stored. A lone private-key header no longer erases the rest of the text in any tool that redacts, prose about API keys is left alone, the JWT and private-key scans are linear (counted, not timed), and `PRIVACY.md` lists what the redactor removes and what it does not. An exporter's `service` label does not name the service in a step; labels set only the namespace and pod, so a forbidden message's verb and user, a pull secret and a claim still come from the text; `diagnose` and the report read one version precedence. An entry that is not a regular file is not listed, read or counted toward the cap, and a file with a second link is not served. The safety contract also fails an unreviewed `Literal`, URL or non-empty list default, and a boolean inside a list of models. Third review: every request body must arrive within 10 s (408); a report slot is taken only after the body is read, on the event loop for REST and MCP alike, so a sender that trickles holds no slot and a waiting report holds no worker thread; a body counts as large only once it is in memory, waits for room until its read deadline, and frees its place when its tool takes it; a refusal on `/mcp` is a JSON-RPC error; an exporter's own pod and namespace are not the subject, `exported_*` labels are when the plain ones name the exporter, TargetDown names its target; an application's `version` label is not an OpenShift release; a reference to a Secret is not redacted where the text proves it is one (the describe form, `secret '…' key '…'`, `secretKeyRef`), while a plain `password=x/secret` is; an `exported_*` label is the subject whatever the exporter; `--access-key` and cut keys behind log prefixes or escaped newlines are; the safety contract walks dict and Optional models, runs validators, and flags non-first Literal choices and bare hosts | `tests/test_alert_report.py::test_the_report_is_the_diagnose_tools_answer_not_a_second_implementation`, | G275 | **A redirect keeps a stale link working, which is exactly why nothing ever flags it.** G266 repointed what the organisation move broke -- GHCR namespaces, the registry name, cosign identities, `gh attestation --repo` -- because those fail. Everything GitHub redirects kept working and kept telling every reader to type the previous owner: the Homebrew tap URL, `/plugin marketplace add`, the Action's `uses:`, the GitLab include, the badges, PyPI's project URLs, the image labels, the chart's `home` and `sources`, the formula's `homepage`, the MCP `website_url`, the `User-Agent`, and the knowledge sources -- 88 occurrences in 35 files, and every new file copied from an old one would have carried it on. Repointing was a classification, not a search-and-replace, because some of those strings name an artifact rather than a repository, and an artifact does not move with the repository. Verified against the live state on 2026-11-11 rather than assumed: the release run logs show images and charts 0.7.0 and 0.7.1 pushed to the previous owner's GHCR namespace and 0.9.0 to 0.9.2 to the organisation's; PyPI's provenance names the previous repository as publisher for 0.7.1 and the organisation's for 0.9.2; the 0.9.2 attestation certificate names `kube-noob/kube-noob`; and the repository's attestation API answers 404 for the 0.7.1 wheel, so `gh attestation verify --repo kube-noob/kube-noob`, which `SECURITY.md` gave for every release, cannot verify a pre-move one. `SECURITY.md` now gives the pre-move `cosign verify` and the PyPI provenance as the exception, and the current commands as the rule. People (CODEOWNERS, the catalog `OWNERS` file and the fork its pull request comes from), pre-move artifacts, history and the guards that refuse the old owner stay, each with its reason in an allowlist the test reads; the test fails on any other occurrence in any tracked file and on an allowlist entry that no longer matches, so the list cannot rot into a blanket exemption. The Homebrew formula and the plugin's server copy were regenerated by their scripts. Two things stay unverified and are not claimed: whether the site still names the previous owner (neither the site repository nor its pages were reachable), and whether a `homebrew-kube-noob` repository exists -- which does not matter, because the two-argument `brew tap` with an explicit URL never looks for one | `tests/test_distribution.py::test_no_tracked_file_names_the_owner_the_project_moved_out_of`, `tests/test_distribution.py::test_the_commands_a_user_copies_name_the_organisation`, `tests/test_distribution.py::test_homebrew_install_is_documented_tested_and_never_ahead_of_the_package`, `tests/test_distribution.py::test_the_old_owner_allowlist_does_not_excuse_a_new_line_of_the_same_shape` |
| G276 | **A plugin that can only describe the engine is not the product, and a skill that answers without it is the failure this project exists to stop.** The directory takes Markdown, JSON and images from an organization that is not Verified and refuses a local MCP server, so `kube-noob-skills` could not run one audit. Read against Anthropic's published rules on 2026-11-12, three designs were admissible: skills that drive the CLI the user installed through Claude's Bash tool ("a script that `SKILL.md` only tells Claude to run isn't part of this check"); a remote server at the user's own `kube-noob serve` through `userConfig`, which Claude Code reports as `Invalid MCP server config ... URL is unset or invalid` on every install that never runs `serve`; and hooks, `bin/` or `!`-command lines, which chat and Cowork refuse or the validator follows as code. The first was built; the second is written up for the owner. An adversarial review of the first version then found twenty defects, and the worst were the project's own thesis in new clothes. A crash exited 1, which is "findings at or above `--fail-on`": a wrong-typed field (`resources: 1` in a Role) crashed `audit`, `redteam`, `fix`, `vuln threat`, `protocol` and `posture scan`, so a pipeline read a crash as a violation and the skill read it as an answer. A rule that cannot read a field's shape is now not evaluated and named. The hand-written readers outside the rules go through `kube_noob.shape`, which raises one exception naming the field: a red-team scenario then becomes `undecided`, `fix` leaves the document untouched with a manual follow-up, and the threat model and attack paths report the object under `notRead`. Anything still escaping `main` exits 4. The skills read `exit 0` as "nothing found" when most commands exit 0 with findings, and missed half the names commands use for what they did not read. Each skill now carries a per-command table, checked by running every command on a fixture. The CLI's JSON reached the model uncleaned, with its not-read keys after a tool's output cap: `KUBE_NOOB_FOR_MODEL=1` reuses `kube_noob.sanitize`, stamps `knowledgeAsOf` and puts those keys first, and the skills write output to a file and print the exit code. Pasted text went through a fixed heredoc delimiter, so a pasted line could end it and run as a command; it is now written with the file tool into a private `mktemp -d` folder. Ordinary checks counted ids in the data folder; the skills now run with `KUBE_NOOB_LEARNING_ENABLED=false`. `kubectl noob` and the `uvx` fallback could download without consent and draw a reviewer hold; the skills use only an installed `kube-noob`. Every command line is parsed with `parse_args`. On krew: the archive carries `LICENSE`, uses stored gzip blocks so its digest no longer depends on the zlib build, describes no commands, and names the documented tap; the no-krew install stops on a failed digest, and the krew-index steps refuse an archive without `LICENSE` A second review found an absence still read as a pass: a PodDisruptionBudget with `selector: 7` made KN-C009 decline and RT-UPGRADE-BLOCK, reading only KN-C009, called the upgrade path `mitigated`. A scenario now records the rules it reads and is `undecided`, naming them, when one did not run; a rule that fails on one object runs again without it; `posture scan` ranks an object with unreadable `metadata` under a name that says so; a `kind` that is not a string is named as not audited; and the skills need kube-noob 0.9.3, the first release with all of this, and call an older one too old. A third review found the exit 5 fired on every CRD a cluster does not serve, so no live gate could pass: it now fires on what was refused (wrong shape, 403, timeout, not JSON), and on what was absent only with `--require-complete`. It also found the red-team record shared between threads, a PodDisruptionBudget of the wrong shape read as no PDB, an unbounded and order-dependent note, `fix` overwriting `securityContext: 7`, documents no rule reads counted as audited, a Rollout with `spec: 7` dropped, and a phrase starting with `-` parsed as an option; each is fixed and pinned. Merged with G273 and G279, which had rewritten the inventory under it: `Inventory.unread(entry, cause)` is the one rule deciding which of `notRead` and `refused` an entry goes to, every site names a cause from a closed list, a table-driven test drives every site through `posture scan` and `posture reach` and a source check refuses a site that writes to a list directly; a live list is refused when the cluster forbade it, timed out, answered something that is not a list (G279's structural check) or not JSON, and an absence when the backend's own words say the type is not served -- for an aggregated group too, which the verdict still keeps not determined, so the kind gate passes on a cluster with no Routes; `posture reach` carries `refused`, `notReadWithheld` and `refusedWithheld` in JSON and text and its `--require-complete` exits 5 on anything under `notRead`; under `KUBE_NOOB_DEBUG=1` a crash is told from a refusal by the traceback G279's entry point prints, not by an exception | G277 | **The first five minutes must not end in a false all-clear, a missing file or a wall of YAML.** A user-side review ran the documented first-run path and hit four defects before any feature mattered. (1) `known-issues --version 4.16.20` answered `"matches": []` with exit 0 and only a prose note naming the indexed releases, so a caller read "not a known bug": the P6 defect class (a verdict earned by absence) in another table. Every surface that consults the index with a version now carries a three-valued statement (`determined`, `not-determined`, `not-narrowed`) with its reason, in text, JSON, YAML and the MCP result, and a version inside the index with no match is unchanged; exit codes are unchanged, because exit 5 means unreadable evidence and this command family has no `--require-complete`. (2) The README's headline commands named files only a checkout has; `examples copy` writes the bundled ones (exclusive create, no symbolic links, every target checked first) and a test runs the README's own `examples/` commands from outside the checkout after the README's own copy step. (3) `diagnose` ignored `--format text`; it now has a text view as its default, like its siblings, with machine formats byte-identical under their flag, and every top-level field of the result is either rendered or listed as omitted with a reason, so a new caveat cannot be dropped from the text by default (G221). (4) `insight` on text it did not recognise said so and nothing else; it now lists what it recognises from the router's table and a bundled example that works, and guesses nothing A second review found seventeen more: pre-release, nightly, OKD and newer-than-indexed z-stream versions read as covered or silently unnarrowed, the statement and the matcher reading the version differently, consumers (selftest, `build diagnose`) dropping the statement, update advice for releases the index cannot see, a partial `examples copy` on a full disk, invisible and C1 characters in the new text view, and causes called likely that the input never singled out; each is held by a test that also tries the neighbouring input A third pass found the matcher reading the head of a paste that diagnose reads the end of, tool models refusing the versions the matcher had been taught to read, a README fallback that needed an absolute shebang, and team memory's cause and commands missing from the text view; one version grammar now decides on every command and tool -- the one `operators issues --openshift-version` set (R5), taken exactly as typed, so a leading `v` or white space is refused with R5's one sentence rather than read as the version it resembles -- and each change of exit code is listed in the CHANGELOG | | G278 | **A join the data cannot populate is coverage theatre, so R5 shipped as a release-note lookup that says what it cannot decide -- and two reviews found fifteen, then a dozen more, ways it still overstated.** Measured first: 957 records (921 issue ids) for OpenShift 4.20-4.22, keyed by OpenShift release and text; none carries a component, a package or an operator version, and every fix is an OpenShift release, while an operator OpenShift ships is versioned `4.y.0-<build>` and updated by OLM on its own schedule. `operators issues` matches each installed operator's package through `ALIASES`, in two tiers that are never merged: the product's name (`named`) and component names or abbreviations (`mentioned`); an issue that also names another product or a platform component is a mention, with the reason. 72 records (65 issues) are found for 13 packages, 27 records name the product outright. The verdict says whether anything was matched (`matched`, `partly-matched`, `not-matched`), separately from whether the evidence was complete (`partial`, `not-determined`): thirty operators without an alias used to read `assessed` and pass `--require-complete`. `complete` is exactly `not incompleteBecause`, one list, so the flag and its reasons cannot disagree (they did three ways); a cluster the notes do not cover -- not OpenShift, OKD, a release outside the index -- is incomplete with that reason, and a release reason is not added when there is nothing to look up. `allMatched` is true only for `matched`, never vacuously (no installed operator, no OLM). There is no matching exit code: `--require-matched` reused exit 5 (unread evidence) for a gap in kube-noob's own name table and was removed; `jq -e '.allMatched == true'` is the gate. A known issue stays listed at later releases until the fix it lists, including a fix in a later release's z-stream (`known_issues.Index._applies`); open issues come first and truncation is counted per class; a fix is never decided, only compared with the cluster's version; community, certified and marketplace builds are not looked up and other catalogs are unverified; the ClusterVersion named `version` is read even when a version is given, an update in progress searches both releases and is incomplete whether or not a version is given, `releasesSearched` and the one update caveat list only what was searched, a version from `status.desired` says so, a cluster that does not serve ClusterVersion is not OpenShift, and an OKD version is not OpenShift Container Platform; a must-gather whose ClusterVersion is not named `version` stays OpenShift, says so, and keeps its condition findings; the must-gather report, its case draft and the analyser's text decide OKD through the same `operator_issues.okd_version` and say the same `okd_note`, with `knownIssuesVerdict` `not-determined` instead of OpenShift matches; a name a refreshed corpus no longer uses makes the operator not determined. The loader every known-issue consumer shares now checks each record of a local copy and counts what it leaves out; the bundled snapshot loads unchanged. Not a gate, not a vulnerability check: the next step is the local Jira copy's components and fix versions | | G279 | **"I could not look" and "there is nothing there" are two answers, and a substring cannot tell them apart; where anything a selector or a tier needs was not read, the verdict is `undetermined`.** `policy reach --live` decided "not installed" from "not found" anywhere in an error. One classifier (`debug.cluster.classify_read_failure`) now serves every live reader, the operator inventory's included (its 38 review tests unchanged): absence only as kubectl/oc's own WHOLE stderr line `the server doesn't have a resource type "<plural>"` (cli-runtime v1.35.0 `builder.go`), read from the FULL redacted stderr the error carries, not the 800-character message, unless discovery of that resource's own group failed; never for a group an aggregated API server answers for (`AGGREGATED_GROUPS`: aggregated discovery can hide that group's failure, client-go v1.35.0 `memcache.go`, `restmapper/discovery.go`); on the in-cluster backend from the HTTP status and body the error carries, not from text that echoes a path. Forbidden only in the backend's own line, never in an echoed name. A body that is not JSON, not UTF-8, without an `items` list, with items that are not objects, or a named read that is not that object, is a failed read. Every one of these makes the answer `undetermined` naming what was not read: a NetworkPolicy list in either namespace, namespace labels any evaluated selector reads (NetworkPolicy `namespaceSelector`, admin-tier subjects and peers; `kubernetes.io/metadata.name` needs no read), a tier, the CNI. An unknown CNI is not two readings but every combination of v1alpha1 and v1alpha2 enforced or ignored; Calico served but versionless is Calico of unknown version. A workload whose first-ranked kind could not be read is an error unless the kind was named. `cluster_info` calls a cluster Kubernetes only when the API does not serve ClusterVersion, and `platformNotRead` reaches `debug`, `bundle` and `policy reach --live`. The admission lookups keep the last good list per resource and mark it stale. Output: every CLI write goes through `sanitize.visible_lines` at one boundary (text, `--compact`, YAML, GitHub annotations, errors), the HTML report and the Markdown writers through the same helper, a lone surrogate is replaced on YAML input, and `visible` follows one rule that keeps joiners in the scripts that need them and does not depend on the interpreter's Unicode table. **Second review:** documents (`fix`, `policy generate`, YAML, JSON, SARIF) bypass the text cleaner and are escaped losslessly by their encoder, stdout equal to `-o`; the admission webhook skips a cross-object rule over a list it has not read; only the exact `404 page not found` body is "not served" in-cluster; an unread NetworkPolicy list gives directions `evaluated: false, allowed: null`; the CNI is identified (Calico, Cilium, OVN-Kubernetes) or "not identified", never assumed; a list must be a complete `List`/`<Kind>List` of named items in the namespace asked for; escapes become nothing and a removed character inside an identifier is marked `<U+200B>`; `clean` and `visible` are one implementation; `serve`, `mcp` and `admission serve` are not wrapped. **GitHub workflow commands (CP3-Q3):** the boundary also turns `##[` into `## [` and a line-leading `::` into `: :` (`sanitize.CommandGuard`), always; JSON and YAML documents and the files `gate` writes escape the same text losslessly; `gate --github-annotations` is the one deliberate exception. **Third review:** the markers are unambiguous and always on (`##<U+005B>`, `<U+003A>:`), one rule covers Azure's `##vso[` and TeamCity's `##teamcity[`; the `policy generate` header and the Markdown reports are broken like text, every document writer (AdmissionReview, bundle, chart, gate files) escapes losslessly, and YAML quotes only a string a line of which could start with `::`; `watch` is wrapped; an escape inside a name is `<ESC>`; annotations are cleaned before they are escaped; an unexpected error is one line and exit 4; a list answer must name its kind; offline `policy reach` says it used OVN-Kubernetes semantics. **Fourth review:** marking is the default of every cleaner (MCP included) and nothing inside a name is removed without a trace; one set of name characters for escapes and format characters; `one_line` is idempotent; every document writer flushes inside the command, so a closed pipe is exit 141.
